Notes on rate equations in nonlinear continuum mechanics
Daniel Aubram

Abstract




Abstract:
The paper gives an introduction to rate
equations in nonlinear continuum mechanics which should obey
specific transformation rules. Emphasis is placed on the
geometrical nature of the operations involved in order to clarify
the different concepts. The paper is particularly concerned with
common classes of constitutive equations based on corotational
stress rates and their proper implementation in time for solving
initial boundary value problems. Hypoelastic simple shear is
considered as an example application for the derived theory and
algorithms.

Keywords:
constitutive equation, corotational rate,
objectivity, large deformation, stress integration, hypoelastic, simple shear

1
Introduction

Many problems in physics and engineering science can be formalized
as a set of balance equations for the quantity of interest subject
to a number of initial and/or boundary conditions. Additional
closure relations are often required which connect the primary
unknowns with the dependent variables and render the set of
equations mathematically well-posed. The most important closure
relations in continuum mechanics

[
29
,
65
,
66
,
93
,
122
,
121
,
48
,
42
]
are
employed to determine the state of stress from the state of strain
and are referred to as the constitutive equations. Rate
constitutive equations describe the rate of change of stress as a
function of the strain rate and a set of state variables.

The choice of a reference system to formulate the problem under
consideration is a matter of convenience and, from a formal
viewpoint, all reference systems are equivalent. There are in fact
preferred systems in nonlinear continuum mechanics, particularly
the one being fixed in space (Eulerian or spatial description),
and the other using fixed coordinates assigned to the particles of
the material body in a certain configuration in space (Lagrangian
or material description)
[
65
,
120
]
. Lagrangian
coordinate lines are convected during the motion of the body, and
referring to them leads to the convected description

[
66
,
104
]
. The arbitrary Lagrangian-Eulerian (ALE)
formulation is an attempt to generalize the material and spatial
viewpoints and to combine their advantages

[
45
,
123
,
14
,
15
,
5
,
7
,
8
,
9
]
.
The equivalence of reference systems for all these descriptions
requires that each term of the governing equations represents an
honest tensor field which transforms according to the
transformation between the reference systems —a property
referred to as objectivity or, more generally, covariance

[
29
,
66
]
. A covariant formulation of continuum mechanics,
in the stricter sense, takes up the geometric point of view that does not
rely on a linear Euclidian space
[
132
,
31
,
95
]
.

As an illustrating example of different reference systems, consider a bar in simple tension which undergoes a
rigid rotation. Then in a fixed spatial (i.e. Eulerian) reference
system the stress field transforms objectively if its components
transform with the matrix of that rigid rotation. In a Lagrangian
reference system, on the other hand, the stress components remain
unaffected by such rigid motion because it does not stretch
material lines. For reasons of consistency it is required that, if
the stress transforms objectively under rigid motions, the
constitutive equation should transform accordingly. This claim is
commonly referred to as material frame indifference

[
79
,
83
,
121
]
and has been the focus of much
controversy during the last decades

[
18
,
92
,
112
]
.

Further complexity is introduced if time derivatives are involved,
as in rate constitutive equations, because both the regarded
quantity and the reference system are generally time-dependent.
This has led to the definition of countless objective rates of second-order
tensors that transform according to the change of the reference system; see
[
39
,
67
,
68
,
72
,
87
]
for
early discussions. Many objective rates are particular
manifestations of the Lie derivative

[
66
,
92
,
95
]
, but not all
[
32
,
53
]
. Today the most prominent examples include the
Zaremba-Jaumann rate
[
51
,
133
]
and the Green-Naghdi
rate
[
36
]
falling into the category of so-called objective corotational rates.

This paper
1
1

1

This paper is a revised and updated version of a preprint shared in 2017
[
6
]
.
gives an introduction to basic notions of rate equations in nonlinear
continuum mechanics. It is not intended as a review article and does not provide a comparitive study of recent developments in the field. We are
particularly concerned with common classes of constitutive equations based on
corotational stress rates and their proper implementation in time
for solving large deformation mechanical initial boundary value problems.

The structure of the remaining paper is as follows. Section
2
addresses kinematics, stress and balance of
momentum as well as fundamentals of constitutive theory. Various
rates of second-order tensor fields are reviewed in
Section
3
, and classes of constitutive equations that
employ such rates are summarized in Section
4
.
Section
5
derives rate forms of virtual power
which are implemented in nonlinear finite element methods to solve
mechanical initial boundary value problems. In Section
6
we discuss procedures to integrate rate
equations of second-order tensors over a finite time interval. We also provide detailed
derivations of two widely-used numerical integration algorithms
that retain the property of objectivity on a discrete level.
Applications of theory and algorithms are presented in
Section
7
using the popular example of hypoelastic simple
shear. The paper closes in Section
8
with some concluding
remarks. Since we make extensive use of geometrical concepts and
notions which have not yet become standard practice in continuum
mechanics, they are briefly introduced in Appendix
A
.

2
Continuum Mechanics

2.1
Motion of a Body

The starting point of any study about objectivity and rate
equations in continuum mechanics is the motion of a material body
in the ambient space. As a general convention, we use upper case
Latin for coordinates, vectors, and tensors of the reference
configuration, and objects related to the Lagrangian formulation.
Lower case Latin relates to the current configuration, the ambient
space, or to the Eulerian formulation.

Definition 2.1
.

The
ambient space
,
\mathcal{S}
, is an
m
-dimensional
Riemannian manifold with metric
\boldsymbol{g}
, and the
reference
configuration of the material body
is the embedded submanifold

\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{S}
with metric
\boldsymbol{G}
induced by
the spatial metric. We assume that both
\mathcal{B}
and

\mathcal{S}
have the same dimension. Points (or locations) in
space are denoted by
x\in\mathcal{S}
, and
X\in\mathcal{B}
are
the
places of the particles of the body in the reference
configuration
. For reasons of notational brevity, we refer to

\mathcal{B}
as the
body
and to
X
as a
particle
.
Particles carry the properties of the material under
consideration.

Definition 2.2
.

The
configuration
of
\mathcal{B}
in
\mathcal{S}
at time

t\in[0,T]\subset\mathbb{R}
is an embedding

\displaystyle\varphi_{t}:\;\;\mathcal{B}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathcal{S}

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\mapsto x=\varphi_{t}(X)\;,

and the set

\mathcal{C}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\{\varphi_{t}\,|\,\varphi_{t}\!:\!\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\}

is called the
configuration space
. The
deformation

of the body is the diffeomorphism

\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\varphi_{t}(\mathcal{B})
. The
motion

of
\mathcal{B}
in
\mathcal{S}
is a family of configurations
dependent on time
t\in\mathcal{I}\subset\mathbb{R}
, i.e. a curve

c:\mathcal{I}\rightarrow\mathcal{C},\,t\mapsto c(t)=\varphi_{t}
,
and with

\varphi_{t}(\cdot)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\varphi(\cdot,t)
at
fixed
t
. We assume that this curve is sufficiently smooth.

\varphi_{t}(\mathcal{B})
is referred to as the
current
configuration
of the body at time
t
, and
x=\varphi_{t}(X)
is
the
current location
of the particle
X
.

Definition 2.3
.

The differentiable atlas of
\mathcal{S}
consists of charts

(\mathcal{V},\sigma)
, where
\mathcal{V}(x)\subset\mathcal{S}

is a neighborhood of
x\in\mathcal{S}
and

\sigma(x)=\{x^{1},\ldots,x^{m}\}_{x}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\{x^{i}\}_{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
.
The holonomic basis of the tangent space at
x
is

\left\{\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{i}}\right\}_{x}\in T_{x}\mathcal{S}
,
\left\{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}x^{i}\right\}_{x}\in T^{\ast}_{x}\mathcal{S}
is its dual in the cotangent space, and the
metric coefficients on
\mathcal{S}
are

g_{ij}(x)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\left\langle\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{i}},\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{j}}\right\rangle_{x}=\boldsymbol{g}\!\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{i}}(x),\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{j}}(x)\right)

at every
x\in\mathcal{V}
, taken with respect to the local
coordinates
\{x^{i}\}_{x}
. The torsion-free connection
\boldsymbol{\nabla}

has coefficients denoted by

\gamma^{\phantom{i}j\phantom{k}}_{i\phantom{j}k}
.

Definition 2.4
.

The charts of neighborhoods
\mathcal{U}(X)\subset\mathcal{B}

are denoted by
(\mathcal{U},\beta)
, with local coordinate
functions
\beta(X)=\{X^{I}\}_{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Therefore,

\left\{\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}X^{I}}\right\}_{X}\in T_{X}\mathcal{B}
is the holonomic basis
X
, and the dual basis
is
\{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}X^{I}\}_{X}\in T^{\ast}_{X}\mathcal{B}
. The metric of the
ambient space induces a metric on
\mathcal{B}
, with metric
coefficients

G_{IJ}(X)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\left\langle\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}X^{I}},\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}X^{J}}\right\rangle_{X}

at every
X\in\mathcal{U}\subset\mathcal{B}
.

Definition 2.5
.

In accordance with Definition
A.6
, the
localization
of the motion
his the map

\left.\sigma\circ\varphi_{t}\circ\beta^{-1}\right|_{\beta\left(\varphi_{t}^{-1}(\mathcal{V})\cap\mathcal{U}\right)}\,,

with
\varphi_{t}^{-1}(\mathcal{V})\cap\mathcal{U}
assumed
non-empty, and

\varphi_{t}^{i}(X^{I})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(x^{i}\circ\varphi_{t}\circ\beta^{-1})(X^{I})

are the spatial coordinates associated with that localization.

Definition 2.6
.

It is assumed that both
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{S}
are
oriented with the same orientation, and their volume densities be

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}V}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}v}
, respectively. The relative volume
change is given by Proposition
A.14
, that is,

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}v}\circ\varphi=J\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}V}\;,

where
J(X,t)
is the Jacobian of the motion
\varphi
.

Definition 2.7
.

Let
\varphi_{t}
be a continuously differentiable,
i.e.
C^{1}
-motion of
\mathcal{B}
in
\mathcal{S}
, then

\boldsymbol{V}_{\!t}(X)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\frac{\partial\varphi_{t}}{\partial t}(X)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\left.\frac{\partial\varphi_{t}^{i}}{\partial t}\right|_{\beta(X)}\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{i}}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}V^{i}_{t}(X)\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{i}}(x)

is called the
Lagrangian
or
material velocity field
over
\varphi_{t}
at
X
, where
x=\varphi_{t}(X)
,

\boldsymbol{V}_{\!t}(X)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\boldsymbol{V}(X,t)
for
t

being fixed, and,
\boldsymbol{V}_{\!t}:\mathcal{B}\rightarrow T\mathcal{S}
. Provided that
\varphi_{t}
is also regular, the

spatial
or
Eulerian velocity field of
\varphi_{t}
is
defined through

\boldsymbol{v}_{t}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\boldsymbol{V}_{\!t}\circ\varphi_{t}^{-1}:\quad\varphi_{t}(\mathcal{B})\rightarrow T\mathcal{S}\;,

so that
\boldsymbol{v}_{t}
is the “instantaneous” velocity at

x\in\varphi_{t}(\mathcal{B})\subset\mathcal{S}
, and

\boldsymbol{V}(X,t)=\boldsymbol{v}(\varphi(X,t),t)
. By abuse of language, both

\boldsymbol{V}
and
\boldsymbol{v}
are occasionally called the
material
velocity
in order to distinguish it from other, non-material
velocity fields.

Definition 2.8
.

Depending on whether
x=\varphi(X,t)\in\mathcal{S}
or

X\in\mathcal{B}
serve as the independent variables describing a
physical field, one refers to

q_{t}:\varphi_{t}(\mathcal{B})\rightarrow T^{r}_{s}(\mathcal{S})
as the

Eulerian
or
spatial formulation
and to

Q_{t}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(q_{t}\circ\varphi_{t}):\mathcal{B}\rightarrow T^{r}_{s}(\mathcal{S})
as the
Lagrangian
or
material
formulation
of that field, respectively.

Proposition 2.1
.

For a regular
C^{1}
-motion, the Lie derivative of an arbitrary,
possibly time-dependent, spatial tensor field

\boldsymbol{t}_{t}\in\mathfrak{T}^{p}_{q}(\mathcal{S})
along the spatial
velocity
\boldsymbol{v}
can be expressed by

\mathrm{L}_{\boldsymbol{v}}\boldsymbol{t}_{t}=\varphi_{t}\!\Uparrow\!\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}(\varphi_{t}\!\Downarrow\!\boldsymbol{t}_{t})\,.

Proof.

By Definition
A.35
,

\mathrm{L}_{\boldsymbol{v}}(\boldsymbol{t}_{t})=\psi_{t,s}\!\Uparrow\!\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}(\psi_{t,s}\!\Downarrow\!\boldsymbol{t}_{t})
, where
\psi_{t,s}
, with

s,t\in[t_{0},T]\subset\mathbb{R}
, is the time-dependent flow
generated by the spatial velocity on
\mathcal{S}

(Definition
A.34
). By Definition
2.7
,
the latter is obtained from

\displaystyle\psi_{t,s}=\varphi_{t}\circ\varphi_{s}^{-1}:\quad\mathcal{S}\supset\varphi_{s}(\mathcal{B})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\varphi_{t}(\mathcal{B})\subset\mathcal{S}\,.

The assertion follows by applying the chain rule for pushforward
and pullback (Proposition
A.5
), and noting that

\left(\varphi_{s}\!\Downarrow\right)^{-1}=\left(\varphi^{-1}_{s}\right)\!\Downarrow=\varphi_{s}\!\Uparrow
.
∎

Proposition 2.2
.

\frac{\partial J}{\partial t}=J\,(\mathrm{tr}\,\boldsymbol{d})\circ\varphi\;.

Proof.

\varphi\!\Downarrow\!\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}v}=J\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}V}
by
Definition
2.6
in conjunction with
Proposition
A.14
, so
J\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}V}
is a time-dependent
volume form on
\mathcal{B}
. Hence, from
Propositions
A.15
and
2.1
,

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}V}\frac{\partial J}{\partial t}=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}(J\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}V})=\varphi\!\Downarrow\!\pounds_{\boldsymbol{v}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}v}=\varphi\!\Downarrow\!((\mathrm{div}\,\boldsymbol{v})\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}v})=((\mathrm{div}\,\boldsymbol{v})\circ\varphi)\,J\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}V}\;,

that is,
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}J=J\,(\mathrm{div}\,\boldsymbol{v})\circ\varphi
. Since skew-symmetric
tensors have zero trace,

\mathrm{div}\,\boldsymbol{v}=\mathrm{tr}\,\boldsymbol{l}=\mathrm{tr}\,\boldsymbol{d}
.
∎

Definition 2.9
.

The
material time derivative
of an arbitrary time-dependent
tensor field
q_{t}\in\mathfrak{T}^{r}_{s}(\varphi(\mathcal{B}))
is
defined through

\dot{q}(x,t)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\left.\frac{\partial q}{\partial t}\right|_{x}(x,t)+\left(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nabla}q\right)(x,t)\;,

where
\dot{q}_{t}\in\mathfrak{T}^{r}_{s}(\varphi(\mathcal{B}))
,

x=\varphi(X,t)
, and the term
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}q
is
called the
local time derivative of
q
.

2.2
Deformation Gradient and Strain

Definition 2.10
.

The
deformation gradient
at
X\in\mathcal{B}
is the
tangent map over
\varphi
at
X\in\mathcal{B}
, that is,

\boldsymbol{F}(X)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}T\!\varphi(X):T_{X}\mathcal{B}\rightarrow T_{\varphi(X)}\mathcal{S}
(cf. Definition
A.26
); the
time-dependency has been dropped for notational brevity.

Remark 2.1
.

The deformation gradient is a two-point tensor
(cf. Definition
A.10
) and can locally be represented by

\boldsymbol{F}(X)=F^{i}_{\phantom{i}I}(X)\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{i}}\otimes\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}X^{I}\,,

in which
F^{i}_{\phantom{i}I}=\frac{\partial\varphi^{i}}{\partial X^{I}}
, and
\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{i}}
attached to

\varphi(X)
is being understood. Note that globally,

\boldsymbol{F}:\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\varphi^{\star}T\mathcal{S}\otimes T^{\ast}\!\mathcal{B}
is a two-point tensor field, where

\varphi^{\star}T\mathcal{S}
denotes the induced bundle of

T\mathcal{S}
over
\varphi
.

Proposition 2.3
.

Let
\boldsymbol{t}\in\mathfrak{T}^{0}_{2}(\mathcal{S})
,
\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathfrak{T}^{2}_{0}(\mathcal{S})
,
\boldsymbol{T}\in\mathfrak{T}^{0}_{2}(\mathcal{B})
, and
\boldsymbol{S}\in\mathfrak{T}^{2}_{0}(\mathcal{B})
, then (compositions with point
mappings are suppressed)

\displaystyle\varphi\!\Downarrow\!\boldsymbol{t}=\boldsymbol{F}^{\mathrm{T}}\cdot\boldsymbol{t}\cdot\boldsymbol{F}\in\mathfrak{T}^{0}_{2}(\mathcal{B})\,,\qquad\qquad\varphi\!\Downarrow\!\boldsymbol{s}=\boldsymbol{F}^{-1}\cdot\boldsymbol{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{F}^{-\mathrm{T}}\in\mathfrak{T}^{2}_{0}(\mathcal{B})\,,

\displaystyle\varphi\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{T}=\boldsymbol{F}^{-\mathrm{T}}\cdot\boldsymbol{T}\cdot\boldsymbol{F}^{-1}\in\mathfrak{T}^{0}_{2}(\mathcal{S})\,,\quad\qquad\varphi\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{S}=\boldsymbol{F}\cdot\boldsymbol{S}\cdot\boldsymbol{F}^{\mathrm{T}}\in\mathfrak{T}^{2}_{0}(\mathcal{S})\,.

Proof.

By Definitions
2.10
,
A.28
, and
A.29
.
∎

Remark 2.2
.

The pullback and pushforward operators involve the tangent map

T\!\varphi=\boldsymbol{F}
, and not
\varphi
itself. This circumstance
would justify the replacement of
\varphi\!\Downarrow
by the
symbol
\boldsymbol{F}\!\Downarrow
, referred to as the

\boldsymbol{F}
-pullback
, and
\varphi\!\Uparrow
by

\boldsymbol{F}\!\Uparrow
, called the
\boldsymbol{F}
-pushforward
.

Definition 2.11
.

The
right Cauchy-Green tensor
or
deformation tensor

is the tensor field defined through

\boldsymbol{C}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(\boldsymbol{F}^{\mathrm{T}}\circ\varphi)\cdot\boldsymbol{F}\in\mathfrak{T}^{1}_{1}(\mathcal{B})
.

Definition 2.12
.

The
Green-Lagrange strain
or
material strain
is
defined by

\boldsymbol{E}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\frac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{C}-\boldsymbol{I})
, in
which
\boldsymbol{I}
is the second-order identity tensor on

\mathcal{B}
, with components
\delta^{I}_{\phantom{,}J}
.

Remark 2.3
.

Note that both
\boldsymbol{C}
and
\boldsymbol{E}
are proper strain measures on
the material body
\mathcal{B}
, and that

\boldsymbol{E}^{\flat}=\frac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{C}^{\flat}-\boldsymbol{G})
, where

\boldsymbol{G}=G_{IJ}\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}X^{I}\!\otimes\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}X^{J}
is the metric on

\mathcal{B}
.

Definition 2.13
.

The
left Cauchy-Green tensor
is a spatial or Eulerian
strain measure defined through

\boldsymbol{b}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\left(\boldsymbol{F}\circ\varphi^{-1}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{F}^{\mathrm{T}}\;\in\mathfrak{T}^{1}_{1}(\mathcal{S})
. In a local chart of
\mathcal{S}
,

\boldsymbol{b}=G^{IJ}g_{jk}F^{i}_{\phantom{i}I}F^{k}_{\phantom{k}J}\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{i}}\otimes\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}x^{j}\;.

The base points have been suppressed. The components of

\boldsymbol{F}^{\mathrm{T}}
are given by Proposition
A.4

Definition 2.14
.

The
Euler-Almansi strain
or
spatial strain
is
defined by

\boldsymbol{e}_{\mathrm{EA}}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\frac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{i}-\boldsymbol{c})
,
in which
\boldsymbol{c}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\boldsymbol{b}^{-1}
is called
the
Finger tensor
, and
\boldsymbol{i}
is the second-order
identity tensor on
\mathcal{S}
with components

\delta^{i}_{\phantom{i}j}
.

Proposition 2.4
.

Let
\boldsymbol{g}\in\mathfrak{T}^{0}_{2}(\mathcal{S})
be the spatial
metric, and
\varphi
a regular configuration, then (i)

\boldsymbol{C}^{\flat}=\varphi\!\Downarrow\!\boldsymbol{g}
, (ii)

\varphi\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{G}=\boldsymbol{c}^{\flat}
, and (iii)

\varphi\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{E}^{\flat}=\boldsymbol{e}_{\mathrm{EA}}^{\flat}
.

Proof.

The proofs of (i) and (ii) can be done in local coordinates with
the aid of the formulas presented in this section;
cf.
[
4
]
for details. From this, (iii) becomes

\varphi\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{E}^{\flat}=\tfrac{1}{2}(\varphi\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{C}^{\flat}-\varphi\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{G})=\tfrac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{g}-\boldsymbol{c}^{\flat})=\boldsymbol{e}_{\mathrm{EA}}^{\flat}\,.

∎

Remark 2.4
.

It should be emphasized that associated tensors are different
objects. For brevity, however, the same name is used for all of
them; e.g. all
\boldsymbol{C}
,
\boldsymbol{C}^{\flat}
, and
\boldsymbol{C}^{\sharp}

denote the right Cauchy-Green tensor. We note that, given a configuration
\varphi
, the tensor
\boldsymbol{C}^{\flat}

plays a role of a material metric induced by the spatial metric

\boldsymbol{g}
, whereas
\boldsymbol{c}^{\flat}

plays a role of a spatial metric induced by the material metric

\boldsymbol{G}
. Quite recently, Fiala
[
33
]
and Kolev and Desmorat
[
53
]
have brought the significance of
\boldsymbol{C}^{\flat}
and
\boldsymbol{c}^{\flat}
in the theory of finite deformations and the definition of objective rates into focus.

Definition 2.15
.

If
\varphi:\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
is a regular
configuration, then the deformation gradient has a unique

right polar decomposition

\boldsymbol{F}=\boldsymbol{R}\cdot\boldsymbol{U}
, and a
unique
left polar decomposition

\boldsymbol{F}=\boldsymbol{V}\!\cdot\!\boldsymbol{R}
. The two-point tensor

\boldsymbol{R}(X):T_{X}\mathcal{B}\rightarrow T_{x}\mathcal{S}
, where

x=\varphi(X)
, includes the rotatory part of the deformation and
is proper orthogonal, that is,
\boldsymbol{R}^{-1}=\boldsymbol{R}^{\mathrm{T}}

resp.
\det\boldsymbol{R}=+1
. The
right stretch tensor

\boldsymbol{U}(X):T_{X}\mathcal{B}\rightarrow T_{X}\mathcal{B}
and the

left stretch tensor

\boldsymbol{V}(x):T_{x}\mathcal{S}\rightarrow T_{x}\mathcal{S}
are symmetric and positive definite for every

X\in\mathcal{B}
and
x\in\mathcal{S}
, respectively.

Remark 2.5
.

It will be usually clear from the context whether
\boldsymbol{V}
denotes
the left stretch tensor or the material velocity, respectively,
whether
\boldsymbol{U}
denotes the right stretch tensor or the material
displacement.

Recall that pushforward and pullback of a tensor generally do not commute with index raising and lowering (Remark
A.9
). This undesirable feature, however, disappears if
the alteration of the pushed (pulled) tensor is carried out according to the pushed (pulled) metric tensor, as already mentioned in
[
66
, p. 70]
and
[
91
, sect. 6.2]
and considered in more detail in the following propositions.

Proposition 2.5
.

Let
\varphi:\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
be a configuration,
\boldsymbol{t}\in\mathfrak{T}^{\phantom{1}1}_{1\phantom{1}}(\mathcal{S})
be a mixed second-order spatial tensor, and let the pulled tensor with all indices lowered be obtained through a pulled index lowering operator defined by the pulled spatial metric,

(\varphi\!\Downarrow\!\boldsymbol{t})^{\Downarrow\flat}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(\varphi\!\Downarrow\!\boldsymbol{t})\cdot(\varphi\!\Downarrow\!\boldsymbol{g})=(\varphi\!\Downarrow\!\boldsymbol{t})\cdot\boldsymbol{C}^{\flat}\;.

Then, the following commutative rule holds:

(\varphi\!\Downarrow\!\boldsymbol{t})^{\Downarrow\flat}=\varphi\!\Downarrow\!(\boldsymbol{t}^{\flat})\qquad\in\mathfrak{T}^{0}_{2}(\mathcal{B})\;.

Proof.

Direct application of Proposition
2.3
, without indicating point mapping compositions, yields

(\varphi\!\Downarrow\!\boldsymbol{t})^{\Downarrow\flat}=(\varphi\!\Downarrow\!\boldsymbol{t})\cdot(\varphi\!\Downarrow\!\boldsymbol{g})=(\boldsymbol{F}^{\mathrm{T}}\cdot\boldsymbol{t}\cdot\boldsymbol{F}^{-\mathrm{T}})\cdot(\boldsymbol{F}^{\mathrm{T}}\cdot\boldsymbol{g}\cdot\boldsymbol{F})=\boldsymbol{F}^{\mathrm{T}}\cdot(\boldsymbol{t}\cdot\boldsymbol{g})\cdot\boldsymbol{F}=\varphi\!\Downarrow\!(\boldsymbol{t}^{\flat})\,.

∎

Proposition 2.6
.

Both
\boldsymbol{R}
-pushforward and
\boldsymbol{R}
-pullback always commute with index
raising and index lowering,
e.g.
\boldsymbol{R}\!\Uparrow\!(\boldsymbol{T}^{\flat})=(\boldsymbol{R}\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{T})^{\flat}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
2.3
and noting that
\boldsymbol{R}
is
orthogonal, i.e.
\boldsymbol{R}^{-1}=\boldsymbol{R}^{\mathrm{T}}
.
∎

Definition 2.16
.

The
Lagrangian
or
material logarithmic strain
is
defined through the spectral decomposition

\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\ln\boldsymbol{U}=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{m}\,(\ln\lambda_{\alpha})\,\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{(\alpha)}\!\otimes\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{(\alpha)}\quad\in\mathfrak{T}^{1}_{1}(\mathcal{B})\,,

where
\lambda_{\alpha}
and
\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\alpha}
, with

\alpha\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
, are the eigenvalues and principal axes of
the right stretch tensor
\boldsymbol{U}
, respectively. The eigenvalues play the
role of
principal stretches
. The
Eulerian
or

spatial logarithmic strain
reads

\boldsymbol{e}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\ln\boldsymbol{V}=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{m}\,(\ln\lambda_{\alpha})\,\boldsymbol{\psi}_{(\alpha)}\!\otimes\boldsymbol{\psi}_{(\alpha)}\quad\in\mathfrak{T}^{1}_{1}(\varphi(\mathcal{B}))\,,

where
\boldsymbol{\psi}_{\alpha}=\boldsymbol{R}\cdot\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\!\alpha}
are the
principal axes of
\boldsymbol{V}
. In the literature, the Eulerian
logarithmic strain is often referred to as the
Hencky
strain
.

Definition 2.17
.

For
m=3
, the tupels of the three principal axes
\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\alpha}
and
\boldsymbol{\psi}_{\alpha}
, with
\alpha\in\boldsymbol{\{}1,2,3\}
, are also called the
Lagrangian triad

and
Eulerian triad
, respectively. Given a Cartesian coordinate system, define the matrices
\boldsymbol{\Psi}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{1},\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{2},\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{3})
and
\boldsymbol{\psi}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{1},\boldsymbol{\psi}_{2},\boldsymbol{\psi}_{3})
as well as the diagonal matrix
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})
, then the
component matrices of the spectral representations
of the right stretch tensor and left stretch tensor, respectively, can be written
[
66
]

\boldsymbol{U}=\boldsymbol{\Psi}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\,\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{\mathrm{T}}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\boldsymbol{V}=\boldsymbol{\psi}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\,\boldsymbol{\psi}^{\mathrm{T}}\,.

Remark 2.6
.

Be aware of the notation involved in Definition
2.17
above.
\boldsymbol{\Psi}
is a matrix
whose columns arrange the components of the

\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\!\alpha}
in the
chosen Cartesian coordinate system
\{Z^{A}\}
, with
\alpha,A\in\boldsymbol{\{}1,2,3\}
. Consequently,

\boldsymbol{\Psi}=\left(\frac{\partial\Psi^{\alpha}}{\partial Z^{A}}\right)

is the Jacobian matrix of the transformation

Z^{A}\mapsto\Psi^{\alpha}
, in which
\{\Psi^{\alpha}\}
denotes the
coordinate system spanned by the principal axes

\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\!\alpha}
. The basis vectors
\boldsymbol{E}_{A}
associated with
the
Z^{A}
are thus given by

\boldsymbol{E}_{A}=\frac{\partial\Psi^{\alpha}}{\partial Z^{A}}\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\!\alpha}
.

Proposition 2.7
.

(i)

\boldsymbol{F}=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{3}\,\lambda_{\alpha}\,\boldsymbol{\psi}_{(\alpha)}\!\otimes\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{(\alpha)}
,
(ii)

\boldsymbol{R}=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{3}\,\boldsymbol{\psi}_{(\alpha)}\!\otimes\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{(\alpha)}
,
and (iii)

\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=\boldsymbol{R}^{\mathrm{T}}\cdot\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{R}=\boldsymbol{R}\!\Downarrow\!\boldsymbol{e}
,
where
\boldsymbol{R}\!\Downarrow
is the
\boldsymbol{R}
-pullback
(cf. Remark
2.2
).

Proof.

(i) and (ii) follow directly from the previous definitions, and
(iii) is a consequence of

\boldsymbol{V}=\boldsymbol{R}\cdot\boldsymbol{U}\cdot\boldsymbol{R}^{\mathrm{T}}
.
∎

Definition 2.18
.

Let
\varphi_{t}:\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
be a regular

C^{1}
-motion, then the
Lagrangian
or
material rate of
deformation tensor

\boldsymbol{D}
is defined by

2\boldsymbol{D}(X,t)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\boldsymbol{C}(X,t)=2\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\boldsymbol{E}(X,t)
. The

Eulerian
or
spatial rate of deformation tensor field

\boldsymbol{d}
is defined by

\boldsymbol{d}_{t}^{\flat}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\varphi_{t}\!\Uparrow\!(\boldsymbol{D}_{t}^{\flat})
,
where
\boldsymbol{d}_{t}:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow T\mathcal{S}\otimes T^{\ast}\!\mathcal{S}
is a spatial tensor field for fixed time
t
.

Proposition 2.8
.

\boldsymbol{d}^{\flat}=\mathrm{L}_{\boldsymbol{v}}(\boldsymbol{e}_{\mathrm{EA}}^{\flat})=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\mathrm{L}_{\boldsymbol{v}}\boldsymbol{g}
.

Proof.

By Definition
2.18
together with Propositions
2.4

and
2.1
.
∎

Proposition 2.9
.

d_{ij}=\tfrac{1}{2}(\nabla_{i}v_{j}+\nabla_{j}v_{i})
resp.

\boldsymbol{d}^{\flat}=\tfrac{1}{2}\left((\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{v}^{\flat})^{\mathrm{T}}+\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{v}^{\flat}\right)
.

Proof.

By Proposition
2.8
, Definition
A.31
,
Propositions
A.13
and
A.7
, and noting that the
spatial metric is time-independent.
∎

Definition 2.19
.

The
spatial velocity gradient
is defined by

\boldsymbol{l}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{v})^{\mathrm{T}}=\left(\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\boldsymbol{F}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{F}^{-1}\right)\circ\varphi^{-1}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\dot{\boldsymbol{F}}\cdot\boldsymbol{F}^{-1}\;.

Morevover,
\boldsymbol{l}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\boldsymbol{d}+\boldsymbol{\omega}
,
where
\boldsymbol{d}=\frac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{l}+\boldsymbol{l}^{\mathrm{T}})
is the
spatial rate of deformation tensor (Definition
2.18
) and

\boldsymbol{\omega}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\tfrac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{l}-\boldsymbol{l}^{\mathrm{T}})=\tfrac{1}{2}((\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{v})^{\mathrm{T}}-\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{v})

is called the
vorticity
, with

\boldsymbol{\omega}_{t}:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow T\mathcal{S}\otimes T^{\ast}\!\mathcal{S}
for fixed
t
.

Definition 2.20
.

The
infinitesimal strain
is the linear approximation
(linearization) to the Green-Lagrange strain about a stress-free
and undeformed state in the direction of an infinitesimal
displacement
\boldsymbol{u}
:

\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{lin}}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathrm{LIN}_{\boldsymbol{u}}\boldsymbol{E}=\tfrac{1}{2}((\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{u})^{\mathrm{T}}+\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{u})\qquad\mbox{resp.}\qquad(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{lin}})_{ij}=\tfrac{1}{2}(\nabla_{i}u_{j}+\nabla_{j}u_{i})\;.

2.3
Stress and Balance of Momentum

We are particularly concerned with isothermal mechanical initial boundary value problems
that are governed by conservation of mass and balance of linear
and angular momentum. This section summarizes some basic relations
for which detailed derivations are available in the standard
textbooks; e.g.
[
48
,
65
,
66
,
122
]
. Notations
and definitions of the previous section are used throughout. In
addition, let the material body be in its reference configuration
at time
t=0
such that

\varphi_{0}(\mathcal{B})=\mathcal{B}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad J(X,0)=1\;.

Moreover, we assume that subsets
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathcal{B}

of the material body and subsets

\varphi_{t}(\mathcal{U})\subset\varphi_{t}(\mathcal{B})\subset\mathcal{S}

embedded in the ambient space have at least piecewise

C^{1}
-continuous boundaries
\partial\mathcal{U}
and

\partial(\varphi_{t}(\mathcal{U}))=\varphi_{t}(\partial\mathcal{U})
,
respectively. The outward normals to these boundaries are denoted
by
\boldsymbol{N}^{\ast}\in\Upgamma(T^{\ast}\!\mathcal{B})
and

\boldsymbol{n}^{\ast}\in\Upgamma(T^{\ast}\!\mathcal{S})
, respectively.

Definition 2.21
.

A
Cauchy traction vector field
is a generally
time-dependent vector field
\boldsymbol{t}
on the boundary

\partial(\varphi_{t}(\mathcal{B}))
representing the force per unit
area acting on an oriented surface element with outward normal

\boldsymbol{n}^{\!\ast}
. If the ambient space is the linear Euclidian
space, i.e.
\mathcal{S}=\mathbb{R}^{m}
, then

\int_{\partial(\varphi_{t}(\mathcal{U}))}\boldsymbol{t}\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}a}

represents the
total surface force
acting on the body. The
Cauchy traction vector at time
t
and point

x\in\partial(\varphi_{t}(\mathcal{B}))
is written

\boldsymbol{t}(x,t,\boldsymbol{n}^{\!\ast}(x))=\boldsymbol{t}_{t}(x,\boldsymbol{n}^{\!\ast}(x))\quad\in T_{x}\mathcal{S}\;.

Theorem 2.1

(Cauchy’s Stress Theorem)
.

Let the Cauchy traction vector field
\boldsymbol{t}
be a continuous
function of its arguments, then there exists a unique
time-dependent spatial
\binom{2}{0}
-tensor field

\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{t}\in\mathfrak{T}^{2}_{0}(\mathcal{S})
such that

\boldsymbol{t}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{\!\ast}\,,\qquad\mbox{resp.}\qquad t^{i}(x,t,\boldsymbol{n}^{\!\ast}(x))=\sigma^{ij}(x,t)\,n_{j}(x)\quad\mbox{in
spatial coordinates $x^{i}$,}

that is,
\boldsymbol{t}
depends linearly on
\boldsymbol{n}^{\!\ast}
.

Definition 2.22
.

The tensor

\boldsymbol{\sigma}(x,t)=\sigma^{ij}(x,t)\,\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{i}}\otimes\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{j}}
,
as well as its associates with components

\sigma^{i}_{\phantom{i}j}
,
\sigma_{i}^{\phantom{i}j}
and

\sigma_{ij}
, respectively, are referred to as the
Cauchy
stress
.

Definition 2.23
.

Let
\rho_{t}:\varphi_{t}(\mathcal{B})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be the

spatial mass density
of the body,

\boldsymbol{b}_{t}\in\Upgamma(T\mathcal{S})
the
external force per
unit mass
, and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{t}\in\mathfrak{T}^{2}_{0}(\mathcal{S})

the Cauchy stress, with
\rho_{t}(x)=\rho(x,t)
,

\boldsymbol{b}_{t}(x)=\boldsymbol{b}(x,t)
, and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{t}(x)=\boldsymbol{\sigma}(x,t)

at fixed
t
. Moreover, let
\rho
satisfy
conservation of
mass
such that
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int_{\varphi_{t}(\mathcal{U})}\rho\;\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}v}=0
, then
\rho
,

\boldsymbol{b}
, and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
satisfy
spatial balance of
linear momentum
if

\rho\dot{\boldsymbol{v}}=\rho\boldsymbol{b}+\mathrm{div}\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}\;,

where
\dot{\boldsymbol{v}}_{t}\in\Upgamma(T\mathcal{S})
is the

spatial acceleration field
and a superposed dot denotes the
material time derivative (Definition
2.9
).

Definition 2.24
.

The
first Piola-Kirchhoff stress
is the tensor field

\boldsymbol{P}_{t}:\mathcal{B}\rightarrow T\mathcal{S}\otimes T^{\ast}\mathcal{B}
obtained by applying the Piola transformation
(Definition
A.39
) to the second leg of the Cauchy stress,
that is,

\boldsymbol{P}_{t}(X)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}J_{t}(X)\left((\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{t}\cdot\boldsymbol{F}_{t}^{-\mathrm{T}})\circ\varphi_{t}\right)(X)

for every
X\in\mathcal{B}
, and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\sharp}

being understood.

Remark 2.7
.

In Definition
2.24
, the placement of parentheses and the
composition with the point map are important: as

\boldsymbol{\sigma}(x,t)\cdot\boldsymbol{F}^{-\mathrm{T}}(x,t)
has its values at

(x,t)
, one has to switch the point arguments. In material
coordinates
\{X^{I}\}
, spatial coordinates
\{x^{i}\}
, and by
omitting the point maps and arguments, one has

P^{iI}=J\sigma^{ij}(\boldsymbol{F}^{-1})_{j}^{\phantom{k}I}
. Similar to
the deformation gradient,
\boldsymbol{P}_{t}(X)
is a two-point tensor at
every
X\in\mathcal{B}
, having the one “material” leg at
X
,
and a “spatial” leg at
x=\varphi(X,t)\in\mathcal{S}
.

Proposition 2.10
.

Since
\boldsymbol{t}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{\!\ast}
is the force per
unit of deformed area in the current configuration of the body,

\boldsymbol{T}=\boldsymbol{P}\cdot\boldsymbol{N}^{\!\ast}
resp.
T^{i}=P^{iI}N^{I}
is the
same force measured per unit reference area (or undeformed area),
and

(\boldsymbol{t}\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}a})\circ\varphi=\boldsymbol{T}\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}A}

Proof.

By Definition
2.24
and Proposition
A.17
.
∎

Proposition 2.11
.

Let

\rho_{\mathrm{ref}}(X)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\rho(\varphi(X,0),0)\,J(X,0)

be the reference mass density at time
t=0
,
\boldsymbol{V}(X,t)
be the
material velocity, and

\boldsymbol{B}(X,t)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\boldsymbol{b}(\varphi(X,t),t)
, then
spatial balance of linear momentum (Definition
2.23
) has
the equivalent Lagrangian resp. material form

\rho_{\mathrm{ref}}\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{V}}{\partial t}=\rho_{\mathrm{ref}}\boldsymbol{B}+\mathrm{DIV}\,\boldsymbol{P}\;.

Proof.

Conservation of mass requires

\rho(\varphi(X,t),t)\,J(X,t)=\rho_{\mathrm{ref}}(X)
for all

X\in\mathcal{B}
by Theorem
A.2
and
Proposition
A.14
. Moreover,

\mathrm{DIV}\,\boldsymbol{P}=J(\mathrm{div}\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}\circ\varphi)
by
the Piola identity (Theorem
A.4
).
∎

Definition 2.25
.

The
second Piola-Kirchhoff stress

\boldsymbol{S}_{t}\in\mathfrak{T}^{2}_{0}(\mathcal{B})
, with

\boldsymbol{S}_{t}(X)=\boldsymbol{S}(X,t)
holding
t
fixed, is the tensor field
obtained by pullback of the first leg of
\boldsymbol{P}
, that is,

\boldsymbol{S}_{t}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\boldsymbol{F}_{t}^{-1}\cdot\boldsymbol{P}_{t}=J_{t}\boldsymbol{F}_{t}^{-1}\cdot\left((\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{t}\cdot\boldsymbol{F}_{t}^{-\mathrm{T}})\circ\varphi_{t}\right)\,.

In components,

S^{IJ}=J(\boldsymbol{F}^{-1})_{\phantom{I}i}^{I}(\boldsymbol{F}^{-1})_{j}^{\phantom{k}J}\sigma^{ij}
.

Definition 2.26
.

The
Kirchhoff stress
is defined through

\boldsymbol{\tau}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(J\circ\varphi^{-1})\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}
.

Proposition 2.12
.

\boldsymbol{S}=\varphi\!\Downarrow\!\boldsymbol{\tau}
.

Proof.

By the previous definitions and Proposition
2.3
.
∎

Definition 2.27
.

Let
\boldsymbol{R}
be the rotation two-point tensor obtained from polar
decomposition of the deformation gradient
(cf. Definition
2.15
), then the
corotated Cauchy
stress
is defined through
\boldsymbol{R}
-pullback
(cf. Remark
2.2
) of the Cauchy stress:

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{S}}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\boldsymbol{R}\!\Downarrow\!\boldsymbol{\sigma}\;.

Proposition 2.13
.

\boldsymbol{R}
-pullback commutes with index raising and index lowering,
yielding

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{S}}^{\sharp}=\boldsymbol{R}^{-1}\cdot\left((\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\sharp}\cdot\boldsymbol{R}^{-\mathrm{T}})\circ\varphi\right)=\left((\boldsymbol{R}^{\mathrm{T}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\sharp})\circ\varphi\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{R}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{S}}^{\flat}=\left((\boldsymbol{R}^{\mathrm{T}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\flat})\circ\varphi\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{R}\;,

where
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\sharp}
is the associated Cauchy stress with
all indices raised and

\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\flat}=\sigma_{ij}\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}x^{i}\otimes\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}x^{j}
is
the associated Cauchy stress with all indices lowered
(cf. Definition
A.16
).

Proof.

By Proposition
2.3
again, and noting that

\boldsymbol{R}
is proper orthogonal,
i.e.
\boldsymbol{R}^{-1}=\boldsymbol{R}^{\mathrm{T}}
.
∎

Theorem 2.2

(Symmetrie of Cauchy Stress)
.

Let conservation of mass and balance of linear momentum be
satisfied, then balance of angular momentum is satisfied if and
only if

\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\mathrm{T}}\qquad\mbox{resp.}\qquad\sigma^{ij}=\sigma^{ji}\;,

that is, if the Cauchy stress is symmetric. Symmetrie of Cauchy
stress is equivalent to symmetry of the second Piola-Kirchhoff
stress, i.e.
\boldsymbol{S}=\boldsymbol{S}^{\mathrm{T}}
.

2.4
Constitutive Theory and Frame Invariance

For isothermal mechanical problems governed by balance of linear
momentum (Definition
2.23
) alone, the motion

\varphi:\mathcal{B}\times[0,T]\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
is
generally treated as the primary unknown. The reference mass
density,
\rho_{\mathrm{ref}}
, and the external force per unit
mass,
\boldsymbol{b}
, are usually given. The Jacobian
J
is known by
the knowledge of
\varphi
, hence the current density
\rho
can
be determined from
\rho=J^{-1}\rho_{\mathrm{ref}}
. The
acceleration
\dot{\boldsymbol{v}}
can likewise be derived from

\varphi
; equivalently, the
m
components of
\dot{\boldsymbol{v}}
can
be determined from the set of
m
equations of balance of linear
momentum. Therefore, in three dimensions one is left with six
unknowns: the independent stress components of

\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\mathrm{T}}
. To
close
the set of
model equations, these stress components are usually determined
from suitable
constitutive equations
.

Sets of axioms based on rational thermomechanical principles are
routinely postulated to constrain and simplify the constitutive
equations. These will not be repeated here. Instead we refer to

[
121
]
and the key papers and lecture notes

[
25
,
24
,
23
,
36
,
79
,
80
,
81
,
82
]

particularly concerned with constitutive theory. Additional
citations are given in the text.

Definition 2.28
.

A
relative motion
or
change of
observer
2
2

2

Both are equivalent provided that the different
observers use charts having the same orientation relative to the
orientation of the spatial volume density
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}v}

[
4
]
.
is a time-dependent family of
orientation-preserving diffeomorphisms

\theta_{t}:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
. A
relative
rigid motion
or
change of Euclidian observer
requires that

\theta_{t}=\theta_{t}^{\mathrm{iso}}
is a
spatial isometry

preserving the distance of every two points:

\boldsymbol{g}^{\prime}=\theta_{t}^{\mathrm{iso}}\!\Uparrow\boldsymbol{g}\;,\qquad\mbox{that
is,}\qquad\boldsymbol{g}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{w})=\boldsymbol{g}^{\prime}(\theta_{t}^{\mathrm{iso}}\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{u},\theta_{t}^{\mathrm{iso}}\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{w})\;,

where
\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{w}\in\Upgamma(T\mathcal{S})
,

\theta_{t}^{\mathrm{iso}}\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{u}=(T\theta_{t}^{\mathrm{iso}}\cdot\boldsymbol{u})\circ\theta_{t}^{\mathrm{iso}}
,
and the tangent map

T_{x}\theta_{t}^{\mathrm{iso}}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\boldsymbol{Q}_{t}(x):\quad T_{x}\mathcal{S}\rightarrow T_{x^{\prime}=\theta_{t}(x)}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}

is proper orthogonal at every
x\in\mathcal{S}
by
Proposition
A.6
such that

\boldsymbol{Q}_{t}^{-1}=\boldsymbol{Q}_{t}^{\mathrm{T}}
and
\det\boldsymbol{Q}_{t}=+1
. In
this case
\boldsymbol{Q}_{t}
is called a
rotation
, with

\boldsymbol{Q}_{t}(x)=\boldsymbol{Q}(x,t)
at fixed
t
.

For notational brevity the index “
t
” will be dropped in what
follows. We also refrain from explicitly indicating the dependence
of a function on a mapping; e.g. for a scalar field

f:\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, a map

\varphi:\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
, and

x\in\mathcal{S}
, we simply write
f(x)
instead of the correct

(f\circ\varphi^{-1})(x)
.

Definition 2.29
.

A tensor field
\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathfrak{T}^{p}_{q}(\mathcal{S})
on the
ambient space is called
spatially covariant
under the
action of a relative motion

\theta\!:\mathcal{S}\!\rightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
if it transforms
according to pushforward

\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}=\theta\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{s}\quad\in\mathfrak{T}^{p}_{q}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})\;.

The field
\boldsymbol{s}
is called
objective
(or
frame-invariant
) if the
transformation according to pushforward is restricted to relative
rigid motions
\theta=\theta^{\mathrm{iso}}
.

Definition 2.30
.

A
constitutive operator
\boldsymbol{H}
is understood as a map
between dual material tensor fields. However, it can be
equivalently formulated in terms of spatial fields by using the
transformation rules outlined in the previous sections.
Conceptually, but without loss of generality, the constitutive
response is denoted by

\boldsymbol{S}=\boldsymbol{H}(\boldsymbol{C},\boldsymbol{A})\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\boldsymbol{h}(\boldsymbol{F},\boldsymbol{g},\boldsymbol{\alpha})\;,

in the material description and spatial description, respectively.
Besides
\boldsymbol{S}
,
\boldsymbol{C}
,
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
,
\boldsymbol{F}
, and
\boldsymbol{g}
,
which have been defined in the sections above, the probably
non-empty sets
\boldsymbol{A}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\{A_{1},\ldots,A_{k}\}

resp.
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\}

consist of generally tensor-valued internal state variables (or
history variables).

Remark 2.8
.

From a formal viewpoint, a constitutive operator is a tensor
bundle morphism between dual tensor bundles over the same base
space
[
92
]
. Bundle morphisms formalize mappings between
tensor fields and guarantee that the domain and co-domain of the
constitutive operator are evaluated at the same base point and the
same time instant; see
[
90
,
98
]
for more details on
bundles and morphisms.

Remark 2.9
.

The list of arguments of the constitutive operator in Definition
2.30

is meant conceptually: the stress tensor is a function of a deformation tensor and
some state variables. In the material description, the right Cauchy-Green tensor
\boldsymbol{C}

has been chosen as the deformation tensor. By Propositions
2.3
and

2.4
(i) the pushforward of
\boldsymbol{C}
is a function of the deformation gradient

\boldsymbol{F}
and the spatial metric
\boldsymbol{g}
, which have thus been chosen as arguments
in the spatial description of the constitutive operator. This is similar to the
formulation of the free energy in
[
103
, eq. (3.3)]
. In contrast to that
reference, however, we do not indicate the dependency on the material metric tensor

\boldsymbol{G}
in the reference configuration, which is needed to form invariants from
\boldsymbol{C}

and
\boldsymbol{A}
.

Minimal requirements in the formulation of constitutive equations
are the following principles.

Principle 2.1

(Objectivity (Frame Invariance))
.

Tensor fields in descriptions of constitutive behavior must be frame-invariant,
i.e., they must transform objectively under a change of Euclidian observer

\theta^{\mathrm{iso}}:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
. For spatial tensor fields
\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathfrak{T}^{p}_{q}(\mathcal{S})
,
the principle is expressed by Definition
2.29
:

\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}=\theta^{\mathrm{iso}}\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{s}\quad\in\mathfrak{T}^{p}_{q}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})\;.

Principle 2.2

(Constitutive Frame Invariance)
.

Any constitutive equation must conform to the principle of
constitutive frame invariance (CFI) which requires that material
fields, fulfilling the equation formulated by an observer, will
also fulfill the equation formulated by another Euclidian observer
and vice versa. The principle is expressed by the equivalence of constitutive
response

\boldsymbol{S}=\boldsymbol{H}(\boldsymbol{C},\boldsymbol{A})\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\boldsymbol{S}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{H}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{C}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{A}^{\prime})

for any relative rigid motion resp. change of Euclidian observer

\theta^{\mathrm{iso}}:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.

Remark 2.10
.

CFI has been introduced by Romano and co-workers

[
90
,
92
,
95
]
in the context of a rigorous
geometric constitutive theory. It is intended as a substitute to
the classical, but improperly stated principle of
material
frame-indifference (MFI)

[
83
,
121
]
, which has been
introduced by Noll
[
79
]
as the “principle of
objectivity of material properties”. MFI and the related concepts
of
indifference with respect to superposed rigid body
motions (IRBM)
,
Euclidian frame indifference (EFI)
, and

form-invariance (FI)
, cf.
[
112
,
18
]
, have been
the focus of much controversy over the years, until recently. In
contrast to that, CFI employs basic and properly settled geometric
notions to account for the fact that distinct observers will
formulate distinct constitutive relations involving distinct
material tensors.

Definition 2.31
.

The pushforward of a constitutive operator by a relative motion

\theta\!:\mathcal{S}\!\rightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
is defined
consistent with Definition
A.29
by the identity

(\theta\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{H})(\theta\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{C},\theta\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{A})=\theta\!\Uparrow\!(\boldsymbol{H}(\boldsymbol{C},\boldsymbol{A}))\;.

Proposition 2.14
.

(See
[
92
, prop. 9.1]
) A constitutive equation conforms
to the principle of CFI if and only if the constitutive operator
is frame invariant, that is,

\boldsymbol{H}^{\prime}=\theta^{\mathrm{iso}}\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{H}\;,\qquad\mbox{or
equivalently,}\qquad\boldsymbol{h}^{\prime}=\theta^{\mathrm{iso}}\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{h}\;,

for any change of Euclidian observer

\theta^{\mathrm{iso}}:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

The assertion follows from Definitions
2.29
and
2.31

by a direct verification of the equivalence with the statement of
Principle
2.2
.
∎

The Objectivity Principle
2.1
and the CFI Principle
2.2
require that any constitutive
equation must conform to them. In this work we are particularly
concerned with spatial rate constitutive equations, according to
the following definition.

Definition 2.32
.

A
spatial rate constitutive equation
is understood as a map
between a rate of strain and a rate of stress in the spatial
description. The spatial rate constitutive equations considered
here take the general form

\accentset{\circ}{\boldsymbol{s}}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\boldsymbol{h}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{g},\boldsymbol{\alpha},\boldsymbol{d})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathsfbfit{m}(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{g},\boldsymbol{\alpha}):\boldsymbol{d}\,,

where
\accentset{\circ}{\boldsymbol{s}}
represents any objective rate of
any spatial stress measure
\boldsymbol{s}
satisfiying
(\accentset{\circ}{\boldsymbol{s}})^{\prime}=\theta^{\mathrm{iso}}\!\Uparrow\!\accentset{\circ}{\boldsymbol{s}}
in accordance with Principle
2.1
, and

\boldsymbol{d}
is the spatial rate of deformation tensor. The metric tensor
\boldsymbol{g}

is included since it is needed to form scalar invariants from
\boldsymbol{s}
,
\boldsymbol{d}

and
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
.

Remark 2.11
.

The particular classes of constitutive equations that fall into
the category formalized by Definition
2.32
include

hypoelasticity
,
hypoelasto-plasticity
, and

hypoplasticity
. These will be discussed in more detail in
Sect.
4
.

Remark 2.12
.

The requirement of objectivity (Principle
2.1
) alone is too weak to results in a unique definition of stress rate. In fact, infinitely many objective stress rates satisfying
(\accentset{\circ}{\boldsymbol{s}})^{\prime}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\theta^{\mathrm{iso}}\!\Uparrow\!\accentset{\circ}{\boldsymbol{s}}
could be obtained simply by adding, to a particular definition of objective rate, terms that vanish under any rigid motion
\theta^{\mathrm{iso}}:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
. Therefore, a large amount of literature is concerned with the discussion
and/or development of objective rates

[
39
,
67
,
68
,
72
,
84
,
115
,
118
,
117
,
127
,
130
]
.
The decisive conclusion for a rate,
e.g. in a constitutive equation, could not be drawn from its
objectivity property alone, but has to consider the intended
application of that rate
[
130
]
. This has already been realized by Prager
[
87
]
, see also
[
72
,
39
]
,
who pointed out the desirability of an additional requirement in defining objective stress rates, particularly in the context of elasto-plastic rate constitutive equations. Plastic yield criteria are commonly formulated as functions of scalar
invariants of the stress tensor; cf. Sect.
4.2
. The elastic response, on the other hand, is prescribed as a function between the rate of
deformation and the stress rate. For a composite of both elastic and plastic constituents to make sense,
zero change in stress (vanishing stress rate) should not
change the value of the yield function. Therefore, the following principle should be added; application of the principle
will be exemplified in the following sections.

Principle 2.3

(Stationary Invariants (Prager’s Requirement))
.

Any definition of stress rate
\accentset{\circ}{\boldsymbol{s}}
, where
\boldsymbol{s}
represents any spatial stress measure, must conform to Prager’s requirement
[
87
]
, according to which
vanishing of the stress rate implies stationary invariants of the stress tensor. Let
I(\boldsymbol{s}(t))
be a scalar
function, then the principle is expressed by the equivalence

\accentset{\circ}{\boldsymbol{s}}=\boldsymbol{0}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}I(\boldsymbol{s}(t))=0\;.

Remark 2.13
.

The scalar function
I(\boldsymbol{s}(t))
in Principle
2.3
could be, for example, one of the principal invariants (Definition
A.20
) or the Frobenius norm
\|\boldsymbol{s}\|=\sqrt{\mathrm{tr}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\sharp}\cdot\boldsymbol{s}^{\flat})}
(Definition
A.17
and Remark
A.4
) of the stress tensor. Some scalar functions could be obtained through application of the metric tensor. In these cases, Principle
2.3
could be fulfilled if the objective rate of the metric tensor vanishes,
\accentset{\circ}{\boldsymbol{g}}=\boldsymbol{0}
; see also
[
39
, sect. 5]
. However, other choices for
I(\boldsymbol{s}(t))
are possible which do not involve any metric.

Remark 2.14
.

Prager’s requirement, originally proposed for elastic-perfectly plastic materials
[
87
]
, has been extended to elasto-plasticity with kinematic hardening, which contains an additional stress-like tensor whose rate prescribes evolution of the yield function
[
130
,
20
]
. Accordingly, the principle of stationary invariants is replaced by a more general
yielding-stationarity criterion
, which demands that all tensor rates must be of the same kind of objective rates. Beyond continuum mechanics and plasticity theory, Prager’s requirement has also gained attantion in general relativity and applied mathematics; see, for example,
[
88
,
114
]
.

3
Rates of Tensor Fields

3.1
Fundamentals

In the following sections we inspect the transformation properties
of the common tensor fields in spatial rate constitutive equations
under the action of any relative motion (resp. change of observer)
and under the action of a relative rigid motion (resp. change of
Euclidian observer). In particular, a distinction is drawn between
spatially covariant rates, objective rates, and corotational rates
of second-order tensors.

In accordance with Definition
2.28
, and by dropping the
index
t
in what follows, let

\theta:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
denote a relative
motion, and
\theta=\theta^{\mathrm{iso}}
if the relative motion
is an isometry, i.e. rigid. The tangent map of a relative motion
is generally time-dependent and denoted by

\boldsymbol{F}_{\theta}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}T\theta
, and the proper
orthogonal tangent map of a relative rigid motion is the rotation
two-point tensor field denoted by

\boldsymbol{Q}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}T\theta^{\mathrm{iso}}
. Here and
in the following we assume that both
\boldsymbol{F}_{\theta}
and
\boldsymbol{Q}

are continuously differentiable in time.

Proposition 3.1
.

The deformation gradient of a motion

\varphi:\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
is spatially
covariant.

Proof.

By Definition
2.29
, Proposition
2.3
and
the chain rule,

\boldsymbol{F}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{F}_{\theta}\cdot\boldsymbol{F}=\theta\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{F}\;.

Composition with the point mappings
\varphi
and
\theta
have
been suppressed.
∎

Definition 3.1
.

The
spin
of a relative rigid motion is defined through

\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\dot{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{\mathrm{T}}\cdot\boldsymbol{Q}
.

Proposition 3.2
.

Orthogonality of
\boldsymbol{Q}
implies skew-symmetry of the spin, that
is,
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}=-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{T}}
.

Proof.

By direct calculation,

\dot{\boldsymbol{I}}=\dot{\overline{\boldsymbol{Q}^{\mathrm{T}}\cdot\boldsymbol{Q}}}=\dot{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{\mathrm{T}}\cdot\boldsymbol{Q}+\boldsymbol{Q}^{\mathrm{T}}\cdot\dot{\boldsymbol{Q}}=\boldsymbol{\Lambda}+\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{T}}=\boldsymbol{0}\;.

∎

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
\boldsymbol{l}=\boldsymbol{d}+\boldsymbol{\omega}
be the spatial velocity gradient,
being composed of the spatial rate of deformation
\boldsymbol{d}
and the
vorticity
\boldsymbol{\omega}
(cf. Definition
2.19
). Then,

(i) both
\boldsymbol{l}
and
\boldsymbol{\omega}
are neither spatially
covariant, nor objective, and

(ii)
\boldsymbol{d}
is objective, but not spatially covariant.

Proof.

(i) Spatial covariance is a stronger version of objectivity under
relative rigid motions, thus it suffices to proof that the
velocity gradient is not objective. First, note that

\boldsymbol{F}^{\prime}=\theta^{\mathrm{iso}}\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{F}=\boldsymbol{Q}\cdot\boldsymbol{F}

by Proposition
3.1
. Moreover, by
Propositions
3.2
,
2.3
, and
2.6
,
and Definition
2.19
, one has

\boldsymbol{l}^{\prime}=\dot{(\boldsymbol{F}^{\prime})}\cdot(\boldsymbol{F}^{\prime})^{-1}=(\dot{\boldsymbol{Q}}\cdot\boldsymbol{F}+\boldsymbol{Q}\cdot\dot{\boldsymbol{F}})\cdot\boldsymbol{F}^{-1}\cdot\boldsymbol{Q}^{\mathrm{T}}=\dot{\boldsymbol{Q}}\cdot\boldsymbol{Q}^{\mathrm{T}}+\boldsymbol{Q}\cdot\boldsymbol{l}\cdot\boldsymbol{Q}^{\mathrm{T}}=\boldsymbol{l}_{\theta^{\mathrm{iso}}}+\theta^{\mathrm{iso}}\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{l}\;,

which is clearly non-objective, that is, it does not conform to
Definition
2.29
. Substitution of

\boldsymbol{l}=\boldsymbol{d}+\boldsymbol{\omega}
, with
\boldsymbol{d}
defined as the
symmetric part of
\boldsymbol{l}
, shows that

\boldsymbol{l}_{\theta^{\mathrm{iso}}}=\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\theta^{\mathrm{iso}}}

and

\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}=\dot{\boldsymbol{Q}}\cdot\boldsymbol{Q}^{\mathrm{T}}+\boldsymbol{Q}\cdot\boldsymbol{\omega}\cdot\boldsymbol{Q}^{\mathrm{T}}=\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\theta^{\mathrm{iso}}}+\theta^{\mathrm{iso}}\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{\omega}\;,

which proofs the first assertion.

(ii) A direct consequence of the proof of (i) is that
\boldsymbol{d}
is
indeed objective under relative rigid motions:

\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{Q}\cdot\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{Q}^{\mathrm{T}}=\theta^{\mathrm{iso}}\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{d}\;.

In the case where
\theta:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
is
an arbitrary relative motion, the tangent
\boldsymbol{F}_{\theta}
is
generally not orthogonal. Definition
2.19
and
Proposition
3.1
yield

\boldsymbol{l}^{\prime}=\dot{(\boldsymbol{F}^{\prime})}\cdot(\boldsymbol{F}^{\prime})^{-1}=\dot{\boldsymbol{F}_{\theta}}\cdot\boldsymbol{F}_{\theta}^{-1}+\boldsymbol{F}_{\theta}\cdot\boldsymbol{l}\cdot\boldsymbol{F}_{\theta}^{-1}=\boldsymbol{l}_{\theta}+\theta\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{l}\;.

Therefore,
\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}=\theta\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{d}
if and only if

\boldsymbol{l}_{\theta}\equiv\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\theta}
, that is, if

\boldsymbol{d}_{\theta}\equiv\boldsymbol{0}
.
∎

Proposition 3.4
.

Cauchy stress
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
is spatially covariant while its
material time derivative
\dot{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
is not even
objective.

Proof.

Transformation of the Cauchy traction vector field
\boldsymbol{t}
using
Cauchy’s stress theorem
2.1
and
Proposition
2.3
yields

\boldsymbol{t}^{\prime}=\theta\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{t}=\boldsymbol{F}_{\theta}\cdot(\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{\!\ast})=(\boldsymbol{F}_{\theta}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\boldsymbol{F}_{\theta}^{\mathrm{T}})\cdot(\boldsymbol{F}_{\theta}^{-\mathrm{T}}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{\!\ast})=(\theta\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{\sigma})\cdot(\theta\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{n}^{\!\ast})=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}\cdot(\boldsymbol{n}^{\!\ast})^{\prime}\,.

For a relative rigid motion
\theta=\theta^{\mathrm{iso}}
this
becomes

\boldsymbol{t}^{\prime}=\theta^{\mathrm{iso}}\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{t}=\boldsymbol{Q}\cdot\boldsymbol{t}=\boldsymbol{Q}\cdot(\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{\!\ast})=(\boldsymbol{Q}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\boldsymbol{Q}^{\mathrm{T}})\cdot(\boldsymbol{Q}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{\!\ast})=(\theta^{\mathrm{iso}}\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{\sigma})\cdot(\theta^{\mathrm{iso}}\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{n}^{\!\ast})=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}\cdot(\boldsymbol{n}^{\!\ast})^{\prime}\;,

by using the property

\boldsymbol{Q}\cdot\boldsymbol{Q}^{\mathrm{T}}=\boldsymbol{Q}^{\mathrm{T}}\cdot\boldsymbol{Q}=\boldsymbol{I}
.
Therefore, the Cauchy stress is spatially covariant. However, its
material time derivative is not even objective because

\dot{(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime})}=\dot{\overline{\boldsymbol{Q}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\boldsymbol{Q}^{\mathrm{T}}}}=\dot{\boldsymbol{Q}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\boldsymbol{Q}^{\mathrm{T}}+\boldsymbol{Q}\cdot\dot{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\cdot\boldsymbol{Q}^{\mathrm{T}}+\boldsymbol{Q}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\dot{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{\mathrm{T}}\neq\boldsymbol{Q}\cdot\dot{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\cdot\boldsymbol{Q}^{\mathrm{T}}\;.

∎

The Lie derivative (Definition
A.35
) is a geometric object that has an
important property: if a tensor is spatially covariant, then its Lie derivative also is.

Theorem 3.1

(Spatial Covariance of Lie Derivative)
.

Let

\varphi:\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
be the motion of a
material body
\mathcal{B}
with spatial velocity
\boldsymbol{v}
, and
let
\theta:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
be a relative
motion such that
\varphi^{\prime}=\theta\circ\varphi
is the superposed
motion of
\mathcal{B}
with spatial velocity
\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}
. Moreover,
let
\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathfrak{T}^{p}_{q}(\mathcal{S})
be a spatially
covariant tensor field such that

\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}=\theta\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{s}
, then

\mathrm{L}_{\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}=\theta\!\Uparrow\!(\mathrm{L}_{\boldsymbol{v}}\boldsymbol{s})\;.

Proof.

Let
\varphi^{i}(X^{I})=(x^{i}\circ\varphi)(X^{I})
,

\theta^{i}(\varphi^{j})=(x^{i}\circ\theta)(\varphi^{j})
, and

(\varphi^{\prime})^{i}(X^{I})=(x^{i}\circ\varphi^{\prime})(X^{I})
be the spatial
coordinates
x^{i}
on
\mathcal{S}
arising from the localizations
of
\varphi
,
\theta
, and
\varphi^{\prime}=\theta\circ\varphi
,
respectively. Then, by the chain rule and
Definition
2.7
,

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime})^{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial(\varphi^{\prime})^{i}}{\partial t}\circ(\varphi^{\prime})^{-1}=\frac{\partial(x^{i}\circ\theta\circ\varphi)}{\partial t}\circ(\varphi^{\prime})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial\theta^{i}}{\partial t}\circ\theta^{-1}+\frac{\partial\theta^{i}}{\partial\varphi^{j}}\left(\frac{\partial\varphi^{j}}{\partial t}\circ\varphi^{-1}\right)\circ\theta^{-1}=w^{i}+\left(\frac{\partial\theta^{i}}{\partial\varphi^{j}}\,v^{i}\right)\circ\theta^{-1}\;,

that is,
\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{w}+\theta\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{v}
, where

\boldsymbol{w}
, with components
w^{i}
, represents the spatial velocity
of
\theta
. The rest of the proof can be done as in

[
66
, pp. 101–102]
, which is repeated here for
completeness. By Proposition
A.12
,

\mathrm{L}_{\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}=\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}}{\partial t}+\pounds_{\boldsymbol{w}+\theta\Uparrow\boldsymbol{v}}\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}=\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}}{\partial t}+\pounds_{\boldsymbol{w}}\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}+\theta\!\Uparrow\!(\pounds_{\boldsymbol{v}}(\theta\!\Downarrow\!\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}))\;.

In accordance with Proposition
2.1
, the flow associated
with
\boldsymbol{w}
is given by
\theta_{t}\circ\theta_{s}^{-1}
, for

s,t\in\mathbb{R}
. Definition
A.35
and
Proposition
A.5
then yield

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}_{\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}}{\partial t}+\pounds_{\boldsymbol{w}}\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}+\theta\!\Uparrow\!(\pounds_{\boldsymbol{v}}\boldsymbol{s})=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}(\theta_{t}\circ\theta_{s}^{-1})\!\Downarrow\!\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}_{t}\right|_{t=s}+\theta\!\Uparrow\!(\pounds_{\boldsymbol{v}}\boldsymbol{s})

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\theta_{t}\!\Downarrow\!\circ(\theta_{s}^{-1})\!\Downarrow\!\circ\theta_{t}\!\Uparrow\!(\theta_{t}\!\Downarrow\!\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}_{t})\right|_{t=s}+\theta\!\Uparrow\!(\pounds_{\boldsymbol{v}}\boldsymbol{s})=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\theta_{s}\!\Uparrow\!(\theta_{t}\!\Downarrow\!\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}_{t})\right|_{t=s}+\theta\!\Uparrow\!(\pounds_{\boldsymbol{v}}\boldsymbol{s})

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\theta_{s}\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{s}_{t}\right|_{t=s}+\theta\!\Uparrow\!(\pounds_{\boldsymbol{v}}\boldsymbol{s})=\theta\!\Uparrow\!\left(\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\boldsymbol{s}_{t}\right|_{t=s}+\pounds_{\boldsymbol{v}}\boldsymbol{s}\right)=\theta\!\Uparrow\!(\mathrm{L}_{\boldsymbol{v}}\boldsymbol{s})\;.

∎

Some well-known, spatially covariant, and thus objective stress
rates can be directly obtained from the Lie derivative. Note
that, with respect to spatial coordinates
x^{i}
, the components of
the Lie derivative of the contravariant Kirchhoff stress

\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\sharp}\in\mathfrak{T}^{2}_{0}(\mathcal{S})
are given by

(\mathrm{L}_{\boldsymbol{v}}\boldsymbol{\tau})^{ij}=\dot{\tau}^{ij}-\nabla_{k}\tau^{kj}v^{i}-\nabla_{k}\tau^{ik}v^{j}\,,

where the general coordinate formula of Proposition
A.13

has been applied. From this one obtains the coordinate-invariant
expression

\mathrm{L}_{\boldsymbol{v}}(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\sharp})=\dot{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\sharp}-\boldsymbol{l}\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\sharp}-\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\sharp}\cdot\boldsymbol{l}^{\mathrm{T}}
. A similar relation holds for the covariant Kirchhoff stress

\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\flat}\in\mathfrak{T}^{0}_{2}(\mathcal{S})
:

\mathrm{L}_{\boldsymbol{v}}(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\flat})=\dot{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\flat}+\boldsymbol{l}^{\mathrm{T}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\flat}+\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\flat}\cdot\boldsymbol{l}\,.

Moreover, by recalling that the Kirchhoff stress is defined
through
\boldsymbol{\tau}=J\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}
, and that the spatial form of
Proposition
2.2
is

\dot{J}=\mathrm{L}_{\boldsymbol{v}}J=J\,\mathrm{div}\,\boldsymbol{v}
, one has

J^{-1}\mathrm{L}_{\boldsymbol{v}}(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\sharp})=\mathrm{L}_{\boldsymbol{v}}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\sharp})+\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\sharp}\,\mathrm{div}\,\boldsymbol{v}=\dot{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\sharp}-\boldsymbol{l}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\sharp}-\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\sharp}\cdot\boldsymbol{l}^{\mathrm{T}}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\sharp}\,\mathrm{tr}\,\boldsymbol{d}\,.

Definition 3.2
.

The rates defined through

\accentset{\circ}{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\mathrm{Ou}}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathrm{L}_{\boldsymbol{v}}(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\sharp})\;,\qquad\accentset{\circ}{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\mathrm{Ol}}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathrm{L}_{\boldsymbol{v}}(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\flat})\;,\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\accentset{\circ}{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\mathrm{Tr}}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}J^{-1}\mathrm{L}_{\boldsymbol{v}}(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\sharp})

are called the
upper Oldroyd rate
and
lower Oldroyd rate of Kirchhoff stress

[
84
,
42
]
, respectively, and the
Truesdell rate of Cauchy stress

[
118
,
117
]
.

3.2
Corotational Rates

The tensor rates defined previously are members of so-called
objective non-corotational rates
. This category has remarkable properties (see, for example, Remark
3.5
and
[
42
,
20
]
), but also
some drawbacks, as will be discussed next.

Proposition 3.5
.

The Oldroyd rates and the Truesdell rate do not conform to Principle
2.3
.

Proof.

According to Principle
2.3
, vanishing of each of the Oldroyd rates and the Truesdell rate of stress must keep the stress invariants stationary.
Stationarity of any tensor invariant requires vanishing of the time derivative of the invariant.
By Definition
A.20
, in conjunction with Definition
A.17
and Remark
A.4
, invariants could be formed with the metric tensor. Therefore, it suffices to show that the Oldroyd rates and the Truesdell rate do not vanish for a non-trivial metric. To give an example, consider the stress invariant
\mathrm{tr}\,\boldsymbol{\tau}=\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\sharp}:\boldsymbol{g}^{\flat}
. Stationarity of this invariant requires

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}(\mathrm{tr}\,\boldsymbol{\tau})=\mathrm{L}_{\boldsymbol{v}}(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\sharp}:\boldsymbol{g}^{\flat})=\mathrm{L}_{\boldsymbol{v}}(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\sharp}):\boldsymbol{g}^{\flat}+\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\sharp}:\mathrm{L}_{\boldsymbol{v}}(\boldsymbol{g}^{\flat})=0\;,

which, upon
\mathrm{L}_{\boldsymbol{v}}(\boldsymbol{\tau}^{\sharp})=\accentset{\circ}{\boldsymbol{\tau}}^{\mathrm{Ou}}=\boldsymbol{0}
, requires
\mathrm{L}_{\boldsymbol{v}}(\boldsymbol{g}^{\flat})=\accentset{\circ}{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\mathrm{Ol}}=\boldsymbol{0}
. By Proposition
2.8
, however,
\mathrm{L}_{\boldsymbol{v}}(\boldsymbol{g}^{\flat})=2\boldsymbol{d}^{\flat}
in general. Similarly, the components of the upper Oldroyd
rate of the inverse metric
\boldsymbol{g}^{\sharp}
, by Definition
3.2
and Proposition
A.13
, are

\left(\accentset{\circ}{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\mathrm{Ou}}\right)^{ij}=\dot{g}^{ij}-g^{kj}(\nabla_{k}v^{i})-g^{ik}(\nabla_{k}v^{j})=-(\nabla^{j}v^{i}+\nabla^{i}v^{j})=-2\,d^{ij}\,,

or, in basis-free notation,
\accentset{\circ}{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\mathrm{Ou}}=\mathrm{L}_{\boldsymbol{v}}(\boldsymbol{g}^{\sharp})=-2\boldsymbol{d}^{\sharp}
. Therefore, the upper and lower Oldroyd rates of the metric do not vanish if the flow associated with the velocity
\boldsymbol{v}
is no isometry satisfying
\boldsymbol{d}=\boldsymbol{0}
. A similar result is obtained for the Truesdell rate, because these three rates do not commute with index raising and index
lowering (Remark
A.11
). Consequently, those stress
invariants formed with the metric tensor are not stationary when employing these objective stress rates.
∎

The drawbacks associated with the Oldroyd and Truesdell rates, and other objective rates, are avoided by corotational rates, as discussed next.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
\boldsymbol{s}
be a second-order spatial tensor field continuously
differentiable in time and let

\boldsymbol{\Lambda}=-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{T}}
be a spin tensor, then

\accentset{\circ}{\boldsymbol{s}}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\dot{\boldsymbol{s}}-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\cdot\boldsymbol{s}+\boldsymbol{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{\Lambda}

is called the
corotational rate of
\boldsymbol{s}
defined by the
spin
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
.

Definition 3.4
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(x,t)=-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{T}}(x,t)
be a given
spin tensor for all
x\in\varphi(\mathcal{B},t)
and
t\in[0,T]
,
with
\varphi(\mathcal{B},0)=\mathcal{B}
. Consider the following
evolution equation

\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{R}}}{\partial t}=(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\circ\varphi)\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{R}}\,,\qquad\mbox{with}\qquad\left.\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{R}}\right|_{t=0}=\boldsymbol{I}\,,

where
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{R}}(X,t):T_{X}\mathcal{B}\rightarrow T_{\varphi(X,t)}\mathcal{S}
is a proper orthogonal two-point
tensor for fixed
X\in\mathcal{B}
and each
t\in[0,T]
, such that

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{R}}^{\mathrm{T}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{R}}=\boldsymbol{I}_{\mathcal{B}}
,

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{R}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{R}}^{\mathrm{T}}=\boldsymbol{I}_{\mathcal{S}}
,
and
\det\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{R}}=+1
. Solutions to the problem generate
a
one-parameter group of rotations
to which

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{R}}
belongs, thus
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
is called the

generator
of that group
[
49
,
102
]
.

Remark 3.1
.

From the previous definition the term
corotational
can be
justified as follows. In a rotating Euclidian frame with spin

\boldsymbol{\Lambda}=\dot{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{R}}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{R}}^{\mathrm{T}}

the Cauchy stress is given by

\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{R}}\!\Downarrow\!\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{R}}^{\mathrm{T}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{R}}
.
Then, the corotational rate
\accentset{\circ}{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}

represents the rate of change of
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}
observed in the

fixed frame
where
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
is measured. Clearly,

\accentset{\circ}{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}=\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{R}}\cdot\dot{(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime})}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{R}}^{\mathrm{T}}\;,\qquad\mbox{or
equivalently,}\qquad\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{R}}\!\Downarrow\!\accentset{\circ}{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}=\frac{\partial(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{R}}\!\Downarrow\!\boldsymbol{\sigma})}{\partial t}\;.

Proposition 3.6
.

All corotational rates satisfy Prager’s requirement (Principle
2.3
).

Proof.

A straighforward proof employing the chain rule and the isotropy property of scalar invariants is provided in
[
76
]
.
∎

There are infinitely many objective rates and corotational rates.
Not every corotational rate is objective, and vice versa. Whether
or not a corotational rate is objective depends on its defining
spin tensor. This aspect is worth to be considered in more detail in what follows.

Definition 3.5
.

The
Zaremba-Jaumann rate of Cauchy stress

[
51
,
133
]
is obtained from
Definition
3.3
by setting

\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\boldsymbol{\omega}
, where

\boldsymbol{\omega}=\frac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{l}\!-\!\boldsymbol{l}^{\mathrm{T}})\in\mathfrak{T}^{1}_{1}(\mathcal{S})

is the vorticity tensor according to Definition
2.19
:

\accentset{\circ}{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\mathrm{ZJ}}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\dot{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}-\boldsymbol{\omega}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\boldsymbol{\omega}\,.

Proposition 3.7
.

Definition
3.3
identically applies for all
associated tensor fields
\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathfrak{T}^{1}_{1}(\mathcal{S})
,

\boldsymbol{s}^{\sharp}=\boldsymbol{g}^{\sharp}\cdot\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathfrak{T}^{2}_{0}(\mathcal{S})
,
and

\boldsymbol{s}^{\flat}=\boldsymbol{g}^{\flat}\cdot\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathfrak{T}^{0}_{2}(\mathcal{S})

irrespective of index placement. That is, any corotational rate of
the metric tensor vanishes.

Proof.

We proof this, without loss of generality, for the Zaremba-Jaumann
rate. Keeping the property

\boldsymbol{\nabla}(\boldsymbol{v}^{\flat})=(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{v})^{\flat}
in mind,
then the components of the Zaremba-Jaumann rate of the inverse
metric
\boldsymbol{g}^{\sharp}
are

\left(\accentset{\circ}{\boldsymbol{g}}^{\mathrm{ZJ}}\right)^{ij}=\dot{g}^{ij}-\omega^{i}_{\phantom{i}k}g^{kj}-\omega^{i}_{\phantom{i}k}g^{jk}=-\omega^{i}_{\phantom{i}k}g^{kj}+g^{ik}\omega_{k}^{\phantom{k}j}=-\omega^{ij}+\omega^{ij}=0\,.

∎

Definition 3.6
.

Let
\boldsymbol{F}=\boldsymbol{R}\cdot\boldsymbol{U}
denote the right polar
decomposition of the deformation gradient, with
\boldsymbol{R}
being
proper orthogonal. Similar to the velocity gradient given by the
relation
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\boldsymbol{F}=(\boldsymbol{l}\circ\varphi)\cdot\boldsymbol{F}
, let the
spatial
rate of relative rotation

\boldsymbol{\Omega}
be defined through

\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{R}}{\partial t}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(\boldsymbol{\Omega}\circ\varphi)\cdot\boldsymbol{R}\;.

Choosing the spin

\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\boldsymbol{\Omega}
in
Definition
3.3
then yields the

Green-Naghdi rate of Cauchy stress

[
36
]
:

\accentset{\circ}{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\mathrm{GN}}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\dot{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}-\boldsymbol{\Omega}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\boldsymbol{\Omega}\;.

Proposition 3.8
.

Vorticity
\boldsymbol{\omega}=\frac{1}{2}(\boldsymbol{l}-\boldsymbol{l}^{\mathrm{T}})

associated with the Zaremba-Jaumann rate and spatial rate of
rotation
\boldsymbol{\Omega}=\dot{\boldsymbol{R}}\cdot\boldsymbol{R}^{\mathrm{T}}

associated with the Green-Naghdi rate are related by

\boldsymbol{\omega}=\boldsymbol{\Omega}+\tfrac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{R}\cdot(\dot{\boldsymbol{U}}\cdot\boldsymbol{U}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{U}^{-1}\cdot\dot{\boldsymbol{U}})\cdot\boldsymbol{R}^{\mathrm{T}}\;.

Proof.

By time differentiation of
\boldsymbol{F}=\boldsymbol{R}\cdot\boldsymbol{U}
.
∎

Remark 3.2
.

The tensor
\boldsymbol{\Omega}
is a kind of angular velocity field
describing the rate of rotation of the material, whereas

\boldsymbol{\omega}
describes the rate of rotation of the principal axes
of the rate of deformation tensor
\boldsymbol{d}=\boldsymbol{l}-\boldsymbol{\omega}

[
26
]
. In contrast to
\boldsymbol{\Omega}
, vorticity contains
terms due to stretching. Therefore, the Green-Naghdi rate
(Definition
3.6
) is identical to the material time
derivative of the Cauchy stress in the absence of rigid body
rotation, while the Zaremba-Jaumann rate (Definition
3.5
)
is generally not. The Green-Naghdi rate requires knowledge of
total material motion resp. material deformation through

\boldsymbol{R}=T\!\varphi\cdot\boldsymbol{U}^{-1}
, while the Zaremba-Jaumann
rate, by virtue of vorticity, is derivable from the instantaneous
motion at current time; in fact
\boldsymbol{l}
is the generator of

\boldsymbol{F}
through
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\boldsymbol{F}=\boldsymbol{l}\cdot\boldsymbol{F}
. This renders the Zaremba-Jaumann rate computationally inexpensive and
particularly attractive to numerical methods that do not store any past
material motion
[
15
,
13
,
5
,
8
]
. By using Proposition
3.8
, it can be shown
that
\boldsymbol{\omega}=\boldsymbol{\Omega}

resp.
\accentset{\circ}{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\mathrm{ZJ}}=\accentset{\circ}{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\mathrm{GN}}

if and only if the motion of the material body is a rigid
rotation, a pure stretch, or if the current configuration has been
chosen as the reference configuration such that

\boldsymbol{F}=\boldsymbol{R}=\boldsymbol{I}
,
\boldsymbol{U}=\boldsymbol{I}
, and

\dot{\boldsymbol{F}}=\dot{\boldsymbol{R}}+\boldsymbol{I}\cdot\dot{\boldsymbol{U}}
; see also

[
121
, pp. 54–55]
and
[
26
,
27
,
22
]
. The
last condition is used in Sect.
6
to compare different
time integration algorithms for large deformations based on the
Zaremba-Jaumann and Green-Naghdi rates.

Proposition 3.9
.

Let
\theta:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
be a relative
motion superposed to the motion

\varphi:\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
of a material body,
then both the Zaremba-Jaumann rate and the Green-Naghdi rate of
Cauchy stress

(i) transform objectively if
\theta=\theta^{\mathrm{iso}}
is
rigid, but they

(ii) are not spatially covariant.

Proof.

(i) Recall that

\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}=\theta^{\mathrm{iso}}\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\boldsymbol{Q}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\boldsymbol{Q}^{\mathrm{T}}
,

\boldsymbol{Q}\cdot\dot{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\cdot\boldsymbol{Q}^{\mathrm{T}}=\dot{(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime})}-\dot{\boldsymbol{Q}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\boldsymbol{Q}^{\mathrm{T}}-\boldsymbol{Q}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\dot{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{\mathrm{T}}=\dot{(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime})}-\dot{\boldsymbol{Q}}\cdot\boldsymbol{Q}^{\mathrm{T}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}\cdot\dot{\boldsymbol{Q}}\cdot\boldsymbol{Q}^{\mathrm{T}}\,,

and

\boldsymbol{Q}\cdot\boldsymbol{\omega}\cdot\boldsymbol{Q}^{\mathrm{T}}=\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}-\dot{\boldsymbol{Q}}\cdot\boldsymbol{Q}^{\mathrm{T}}

by Proposition
3.3
(i). The pushforward of the
Zaremba-Jaumann rate (Definition
3.5
) along the relative
rigid motion
\theta^{\mathrm{iso}}
then becomes

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Q}\cdot\accentset{\circ}{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\mathrm{ZJ}}\cdot\boldsymbol{Q}^{\mathrm{T}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{Q}\cdot(\dot{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}-\boldsymbol{\omega}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}\cdot\boldsymbol{\omega})\cdot\boldsymbol{Q}^{\mathrm{T}}=\boldsymbol{Q}\cdot\dot{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\cdot\boldsymbol{Q}^{\mathrm{T}}-\boldsymbol{Q}\cdot\boldsymbol{\omega}\cdot\boldsymbol{Q}^{\mathrm{T}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}\cdot\boldsymbol{Q}\cdot\boldsymbol{\omega}\cdot\boldsymbol{Q}^{\mathrm{T}}

\displaystyle=\dot{(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime})}-\dot{\boldsymbol{Q}}\cdot\boldsymbol{Q}^{\mathrm{T}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}\cdot\dot{\boldsymbol{Q}}\cdot\boldsymbol{Q}^{\mathrm{T}}-(\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}-\dot{\boldsymbol{Q}}\cdot\boldsymbol{Q}^{\mathrm{T}})\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}+\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}\cdot(\boldsymbol{\omega}^{\prime}-\dot{\boldsymbol{Q}}\cdot\boldsymbol{Q}^{\mathrm{T}})
