\boldsymbol{a}^{\!\ast}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{a}^{\!\ast}_{p},\boldsymbol{b}^{\!\ast}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{b}^{\!\ast}_{r}\in T^{\ast}_{X}\mathcal{M}
.

Proposition A.3
.

A
\binom{p}{q}
-tensor
\boldsymbol{T}
has the local representative

\boldsymbol{T}(X)=T^{i_{1}\ldots i_{p}}_{\phantom{i_{1}\ldots i_{p}}j_{1}\ldots j_{q}}(X)\,\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{i_{1}}}\!\otimes\ldots\otimes\!\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{i_{p}}}\!\otimes\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}x^{j_{1}}\!\otimes\ldots\otimes\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}x^{j_{q}}\,.

and

T^{p}_{q}(\mathcal{M})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\underbrace{T\mathcal{M}\otimes\ldots\otimes T\mathcal{M}}_{p\mathrm{-fold}}\otimes\underbrace{T^{\ast}\!\mathcal{M}\otimes\ldots\otimes T^{\ast}\!\mathcal{M}}_{q\mathrm{-fold}}
.

Proof.

By Definitions
A.11
and
A.13
.
∎

Definition A.14
.

A
Riemannian manifold
is the pair
(\mathcal{M},\boldsymbol{g})
,
where
\mathcal{M}
is a differentiable manifold and
\boldsymbol{g}
is a

metric
. If
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathcal{M}
is a subset and

(\mathcal{U},\beta)
a chart with coordinate functions

\beta(X)=\{x^{i}\}_{X}
for every
X\in\mathcal{U}
, then the metric
can be locally represented by

\boldsymbol{g}(X)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}g_{ij}(X)\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}x^{i}\!\otimes\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}x^{j}\;,\qquad\mbox{where}\quad g_{ij}\,(X)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\left\langle\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{i}}\,,\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{j}}\right\rangle_{X}\geq 0

are the
metric coefficients
at every point

X\in\mathcal{M}
and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is the
inner product
associated with the metric
\boldsymbol{g}
on
\mathcal{M}
.

Definition A.15
.

Contracting the metric tensor with the
inverse metric

\boldsymbol{g}^{-1}=g^{ij}\,\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{i}}\!\otimes\!\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{j}}
,
where
g_{ik}\,g^{kj}=\delta_{i}^{\phantom{i}j}
, gives the

second-order identity tensor on
\mathcal{M}
,

\boldsymbol{I}_{\mathcal{M}}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\boldsymbol{g}\cdot\boldsymbol{g}^{-1}=\delta_{i}^{\phantom{i}j}\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}x^{i}\otimes\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{j}}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}x^{i}\otimes\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{i}}\,.

The
fourth-order symmetric identity tensor
or

symmetrizer

\boldsymbol{\mathsf{1}}_{\mathcal{M}}
, with
components

\mathsf{1}_{ij}^{\phantom{ij}kl}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\frac{1}{2}\left(\delta_{i}^{\phantom{i}k}\,\delta_{j}^{\phantom{j}l}+\delta_{i}^{\phantom{i}l}\,\delta_{j}^{\phantom{j}k}\right)\,,

yields the symmetric part

\mathrm{Sym}\,(\boldsymbol{T})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{1}}_{\mathcal{M}}:\boldsymbol{T}

of a second-order tensor
\boldsymbol{T}\in T^{1}_{1}(\mathcal{M})
.

Definition A.16
.

Let
\boldsymbol{S}\in T^{p}_{q}(\mathcal{M})
, then
\boldsymbol{S}^{\flat}\in T^{0}_{p+q}(\mathcal{M})
is the
associated tensor with all
indices lowered
, and
\boldsymbol{S}^{\sharp}\in T_{0}^{p+q}(\mathcal{M})
is
the
associated tensor with all indices raised
. Here

♭
is called the
index lowering operator
, and

♯
is the
index raising operator
.

Definition A.17
.

The
Frobenius norm
of
\boldsymbol{T}\in T^{1}_{1}(\mathcal{M})
is
defined through

\|\boldsymbol{T}\|\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\sqrt{\mathrm{tr}(\boldsymbol{T}^{2})}
,
where

\boldsymbol{T}^{2}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\boldsymbol{T}^{\sharp}\cdot\boldsymbol{T}^{\flat}\in T^{1}_{1}(\mathcal{M})
is the
squared tensor
\boldsymbol{T}
.

Remark A.3
.

Tensor indices can be raised by the inverse metric coefficients,
and lowered by the metric coefficients. For example, let

\boldsymbol{T}=T^{i}_{\;j}\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{i}}\otimes\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}x^{j}\in T^{1}_{1}(\mathcal{M})
in a given chart, then the associated tensors
are

\boldsymbol{T}^{\flat}=\boldsymbol{g}\cdot\boldsymbol{T}=g_{ik}T^{k}_{\phantom{k}j}\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}x^{i}\!\otimes\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}x^{j}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\boldsymbol{T}^{\sharp}=\boldsymbol{T}\cdot\boldsymbol{g}^{-1}=T^{i}_{\phantom{i}k}g^{kj}\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{i}}\!\otimes\!\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{j}}\,.

Remark A.4
.

Note that

\boldsymbol{g}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\boldsymbol{g}^{\flat}=(\boldsymbol{I}_{\mathcal{M}})^{\flat}

and

\boldsymbol{g}^{-1}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\boldsymbol{g}^{\sharp}=(\boldsymbol{I}_{\mathcal{M}})^{\sharp}
.
Moreover, the trace of
\boldsymbol{T}\in T^{1}_{1}(\mathcal{M})
can be
written
\mathrm{tr}\,\boldsymbol{T}=\boldsymbol{T}^{\sharp}:\boldsymbol{g}
.

Definition A.18
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
,
\mathcal{N}
be Riemannian manifolds and

\boldsymbol{T}(X):T_{X}\mathcal{M}\rightarrow T_{\varphi(X)}\mathcal{N}
a
general two-point tensor over a diffeomorphism

\varphi:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}
. Moreover, let

\boldsymbol{U}\in T_{X}\mathcal{M}
and
\boldsymbol{v}\in T_{x}\mathcal{N}
be
vectors on
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{N}
, respectively, with

X\in\mathcal{M}
and
x=\varphi(X)\in\mathcal{N}
, then the

transpose of
\boldsymbol{T}
is the linear map

\boldsymbol{T}^{\mathrm{T}}(x):T_{x}(\varphi(\mathcal{M}))\rightarrow T_{\varphi^{-1}(x)}\mathcal{M}
defined through

\left\langle\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{T}(\boldsymbol{U})\right\rangle_{x}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\left\langle\boldsymbol{T}^{\mathrm{T}}\!(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{U}\right\rangle_{X}\;.

For
\varphi=\mathrm{Id}
resp.
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{M}
the
transpose of an ordinary (one-point) tensor is obtained.

Proposition A.4
.

The components of
\boldsymbol{T}^{\mathrm{T}}
are given by

(\boldsymbol{T}^{\mathrm{T}})^{\phantom{j}I}_{i}(x)=g_{ij}(x)\,T^{j}_{\;\,J}(\varphi^{-1}(x))\,G^{IJ}(\varphi^{-1}(x))

with respect to local bases

\left\{\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}X^{I}}\right\}\in T_{X}\mathcal{M}
and

\left\{\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{i}}\right\}\in T_{x}\mathcal{N}
, where
g_{ij}(x)
are the metric coefficients on

\mathcal{N}
and
G^{IJ}(X)
are the inverse metric coefficients
on
\mathcal{M}
.

Proof.

By the definitions of the transpose, metric and inverse metric;
see
[
66
,
4
]
for details.
∎

Definition A.19
.

With
\boldsymbol{T}
,
\boldsymbol{U}
, and
\boldsymbol{v}
be as before, the operations

\qquad\boldsymbol{T}^{-1}\cdot\boldsymbol{T}(\boldsymbol{U})=\boldsymbol{U}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\boldsymbol{T}^{-\mathrm{T}}\cdot\boldsymbol{T}^{\mathrm{T}}(\boldsymbol{v})=\boldsymbol{v}

involve the
inverse

\boldsymbol{T}^{-1}(X)
and the
inverse
transpose

\boldsymbol{T}^{-\mathrm{T}}(x)
. Moreover, a two-point tensor

\boldsymbol{T}(X):T_{X}\mathcal{M}\rightarrow T_{\varphi(X)}\mathcal{N}
is
called
orthogonal
provided that

\boldsymbol{T}^{\mathrm{T}}\cdot\boldsymbol{T}=\boldsymbol{I}_{\mathcal{M}}
and

\boldsymbol{T}\cdot\boldsymbol{T}^{\mathrm{T}}=\boldsymbol{I}_{\mathcal{N}}
. If
\boldsymbol{T}

is orthogonal and the determinant
\det\boldsymbol{T}=+1
, then
\boldsymbol{T}

is called
proper orthogonal
.

Definition A.20
.

On a three-dimensional Riemannian manifold, let
\lambda_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
,
k\in\{1,2,3\}
, denote the
eigenvalues
of a second-order tensor
\boldsymbol{T}
.
Given a
3\times 3
-matrix representation of
\boldsymbol{T}
, the Caley-Hamilton theorem states that this matrix satisfies its own
characteristic polynomial

\det(\boldsymbol{T}-\lambda_{k}\boldsymbol{I})=\lambda_{k}^{3}-I_{1}(\boldsymbol{T})\,\lambda_{k}^{2}+I_{2}(\boldsymbol{T})\,\lambda_{k}-I_{3}(\boldsymbol{T})=0\;.

The coefficients
I_{1}(\boldsymbol{T}),I_{2}(\boldsymbol{T}),I_{3}(\boldsymbol{T})
are called the
principal invariants

of the tensor, with

\displaystyle I_{1}(\boldsymbol{T})

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathrm{tr}\,\boldsymbol{T}=\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}\;,

\displaystyle I_{2}(\boldsymbol{T})

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\det\boldsymbol{T}\,\mathrm{tr}(\boldsymbol{T}^{-1})=\tfrac{1}{2}((\mathrm{tr}\,\boldsymbol{T})^{2}-\mathrm{tr}(\boldsymbol{T}^{2}))\;,

\displaystyle I_{3}(\boldsymbol{T})

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\det\boldsymbol{T}=\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}\;.

In what follows,
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{N}
are
differentiable manifolds,

\varphi:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}
is a diffeomorphism,

(\mathcal{U},\beta)
and
(\mathcal{V},\sigma)
are charts of

\mathcal{U}\subset\mathcal{M}
and

\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{N}
, respectively, and

\varphi^{i}(X^{I})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(x^{i}\circ\varphi\circ\beta^{-1})(X^{I})

are the coordinates
x^{i}
on
\mathcal{N}
arising from the
coordinates
X^{I}
on
\mathcal{U}
via localization of
\varphi
.

Definition A.21
.

The tangent bundle of
\mathcal{M}
has been denoted

T\mathcal{M}
. In in a more rigorous definition, it is the
triplet
(T\mathcal{M},\,\tau_{\mathcal{M}},\,\mathcal{M})

including the
projection

\tau_{\mathcal{M}}:T\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
. At

X\in\mathcal{M}
, with
\mathrm{dim}(\mathcal{M})=n
, the tangent
space

\tau_{\mathcal{M}}^{-1}(X)=T_{X}\mathcal{M}=\{X\}\times\mathcal{V}_{n}

is called
fibre over
X
, and
\mathcal{V}_{n}
is the

fibre space
. If

(T\mathcal{N},\,\tau_{\mathcal{N}},\,\mathcal{N})
is another
tangent bundle, then a continuous map

\varphi:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}
induces the bundle

(\varphi^{\star}T\mathcal{N},\,\tau^{\prime}_{\mathcal{N}},\,\mathcal{M})

with
\tau^{\prime}_{\mathcal{N}}:\varphi^{\star}T\mathcal{N}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
. The restriction of

\varphi^{\star}T\mathcal{N}
to
x=\varphi(X)\in\mathcal{N}
is
the tangent space
T_{\varphi(X)}\mathcal{N}
.

Definition A.22
.

A
vector field
\boldsymbol{v}
on
\mathcal{M}
is identified with
the
tangent bundle section

\boldsymbol{v}:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow T\mathcal{M}\,,

with
\tau_{\mathcal{M}}(\boldsymbol{v}(X))=X,\;\forall X\in\mathcal{M}
.
A
1-form field
is a section of the cotangent bundle:

\boldsymbol{a}^{\ast}:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow T^{\ast}\!\mathcal{M}
. The sets
of all sections of
T\mathcal{M}
and
T^{\ast}\!\mathcal{M}
are
denoted by
\Upgamma(T\mathcal{M})
and

\Upgamma(T^{\ast}\!\mathcal{M})
, respectively. As well, if some
manifold
\mathcal{N}
has the tangent bundle
T\mathcal{N}
,

\boldsymbol{u}\in\Upgamma(T\mathcal{N})
is a vector field, and

\varphi:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}
is continuous, then
the related
vector field over
\varphi
is the

induced section

\varphi^{\star}\boldsymbol{u}:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow T\mathcal{N}
defined through

(\varphi^{\star}\boldsymbol{u})(X)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\boldsymbol{u}(\varphi(X))
.

Definition A.23
.

The
local basis sections
of
T\mathcal{M}
restricted to

\mathcal{U}
,

\left\{\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{1}},\ldots,\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{n}}\right\}:\quad\mathcal{U}\rightarrow\left.T\mathcal{M}\right|_{\mathcal{U}}

define a local basis for all
X\in\mathcal{M}
, and

\left\{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}x^{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}x^{n}\right\}:\mathcal{U}\rightarrow\left.T^{\ast}\!\mathcal{M}\right|_{\mathcal{U}}
are their duals.
Hence, for every fibre
\tau_{\mathcal{M}}^{-1}(X)
at

X\in\mathcal{U}
,

\boldsymbol{v}(X)=v^{i}(X)\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{i}}(X)
and

\boldsymbol{a}^{\ast}(X)=a_{i}(X)\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}x^{i}(X)
, respectively. For the
fields to be continuously differentiable, the mappings

x^{i}\rightarrow v^{j}(x^{i})
and
x^{i}\rightarrow a_{j}(x^{i})
on

\beta(\mathcal{U})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
are required to be
continuously differentiable.

Remark A.5
.

One may construct tensor fields of any order by fibrewise
tensor-multiplication of vector and 1-form fields. For example, if

\boldsymbol{w}\in\Upgamma(T\mathcal{M})
and

\boldsymbol{b}^{\ast}\in\Upgamma(T^{\ast}\!\mathcal{M})
, a

\binom{1}{1}
-tensor field
\boldsymbol{T}
would be

\boldsymbol{T}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(\boldsymbol{w}\otimes\boldsymbol{b}^{\ast})\in\Upgamma(T\mathcal{M}\otimes T^{\ast}\!\mathcal{M})\,,

and

(\boldsymbol{w}\otimes\boldsymbol{b}^{\ast})(X)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\boldsymbol{w}(X)\otimes\boldsymbol{b}^{\ast}(X)
.
Thus
\boldsymbol{T}
is a section of the
\binom{1}{1}
-tensor bundle

T^{1}_{1}(\mathcal{M})=T\mathcal{M}\otimes T^{\ast}\!\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
. Two-point tensor
fields over maps
\varphi:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}
are
defined analogously by taking into account the sections induced by

\varphi
.

Definition A.24
.

The
\binom{p}{q}
-tensor bundle of
\mathcal{M}
is denoted by

(T^{p}_{q}(\mathcal{M}),\,\tau_{\mathcal{M}},\,\mathcal{M})
, or
just
T^{p}_{q}(\mathcal{M})
, and the set of all sections of it
is denoted by

\mathfrak{T}^{p}_{q}(\mathcal{M})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\Upgamma(T^{p}_{q}(\mathcal{M}))
.

Definition A.25
.

The index lowering and index raising operators for tensors carry
over to tensor fields by defining the so-called
musical
isomorphisms

{}^{\flat}:T\mathcal{M}\rightarrow T^{\ast}\!\mathcal{M}

and
{}^{\sharp}:T^{\ast}\!\mathcal{M}\rightarrow T\mathcal{M}
,
respectively.

A.3
Pushforward and Pullback

Definition A.26
.

The
tangent map
and
cotangent map
over
\varphi
are
defined through

\displaystyle T\varphi:\,\,T\mathcal{M}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\ T\mathcal{N}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad T^{\ast}\!\varphi:\,\,T^{\ast}\!\mathcal{N}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\ T^{\ast}\!\mathcal{M}

\displaystyle\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}X^{I}}

\displaystyle\mapsto\ \frac{\partial\varphi^{i}}{\partial X^{I}}\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{i}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}x^{i}

\displaystyle\mapsto\ \frac{\partial\varphi^{i}}{\partial X^{I}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}X^{I}\,,

respectively.

Remark A.6
.

If
\boldsymbol{V}=V^{I}\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}X^{I}}\in T_{X}\mathcal{M}
is a vector at
X\in\mathcal{M}
, then

T\varphi\left(\boldsymbol{V}\right)(X)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\left(\frac{\partial\varphi^{i}}{\partial X^{I}}V^{I}\right)(X)\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{i}}(\varphi(X))\qquad\in T_{\varphi(X)}\mathcal{N}\,,

that is,
T\varphi(\boldsymbol{V})=V^{I}\frac{\partial\varphi^{i}}{\partial X^{I}}\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{i}}
without indicating the
base point. As the tangent map is linear, a two-point tensor

\boldsymbol{F}(X)\in T_{\varphi(X)}\mathcal{N}\otimes T^{\ast}_{X}\mathcal{M}
can be defined yielding the same result:

T\varphi\left(\boldsymbol{V}\right)=V^{I}\frac{\partial\varphi^{i}}{\partial X^{I}}\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{i}}=V^{I}\underbrace{\left(\frac{\partial\varphi^{i}}{\partial X^{J}}\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{i}}\!\otimes\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}X^{J}\right)}_{\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\boldsymbol{F}}\cdot\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}X^{I}}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\boldsymbol{F}\cdot\boldsymbol{V}\,.

Hence, we may write
T\varphi\circ\boldsymbol{V}=T\varphi\cdot\boldsymbol{V}
.
Moreover, if
\boldsymbol{a}^{\ast}\in T^{\ast}\!\mathcal{N}
, then

T^{\ast}\!\varphi(\boldsymbol{a}^{\ast})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\boldsymbol{a}^{\ast}\cdot\boldsymbol{F}
.

Remark A.7
.

If
\boldsymbol{V}:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow T\mathcal{M}
is a vector field
and not just a vector emanating from a specific base point, then

T\varphi(\boldsymbol{V})
is a vector field over
\varphi
.

T\varphi(\boldsymbol{V})
becomes an honest vector field on
\mathcal{N}

when the base points are switched.

Definition A.27
.

The
pushforward by
\varphi
of a vector field

\boldsymbol{V}\in\Upgamma(T\mathcal{M})
is the vector field

\varphi\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{V}\in\Upgamma(T\mathcal{N})
defined by

\varphi\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{V}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(T\varphi\cdot\boldsymbol{V})\circ\varphi^{-1}\;.

The
pullback by
\varphi
of a vector field

\boldsymbol{w}\in\Upgamma(T\mathcal{N})
is a vector field on

\mathcal{M}
defined through

\varphi\!\Downarrow\!\boldsymbol{w}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(T(\varphi^{-1})\circ\boldsymbol{w})\circ\varphi\in\Upgamma(T\mathcal{M})
.

Remark A.8
.

The definition carries over to real functions resp. scalar fields.
For example, let
f:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, then the
pushforward

\varphi\!\Uparrow\!f\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}f\circ\varphi^{-1}

is a scalar field on
\mathcal{N}
, and

\varphi\!\Uparrow=\left(\varphi^{-1}\right)\!\Downarrow
defines
the pullback of a scalar field as the inverse operation. Note that

f
has the same values at
X\in\mathcal{M}
as

\varphi\!\Uparrow\!f
has at
x=\varphi(X)\in\mathcal{N}
.

Proposition A.5
.

For a composition of maps
\varphi
and
\psi
,

(\psi\circ\varphi)\!\Downarrow\;=\varphi\!\Downarrow\!\;\circ\;\psi\!\Downarrow\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad(\psi\circ\varphi)\!\Uparrow\;=\psi\!\Uparrow\;\circ\;\varphi\!\Uparrow\,.

Proof.

By the chain rule; see, for example,
[
1
,
66
]
.
∎

Definition A.28
.

The pullback and pushforward of 1-form fields

\boldsymbol{a}^{\!\ast}\in\Upgamma(T^{\ast}\!\mathcal{N})
and

\boldsymbol{B}^{\ast}\in\Upgamma(T^{\ast}\!\mathcal{M})
on
\mathcal{N}

and
\mathcal{M}
, respectively, are being defined according to
their action on vector fields. Clearly,

\varphi\!\Downarrow\!\boldsymbol{a}^{\!\ast}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(\boldsymbol{a}^{\!\ast}\circ\varphi)\cdot T\varphi\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\varphi\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{B}^{\ast}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(\boldsymbol{B}^{\ast}\circ\varphi^{-1})\cdot T(\varphi^{-1})\,,

respectively.

Definition A.29
.

Let
\boldsymbol{T}\in\mathfrak{T}^{p}_{q}(\mathcal{M})
and

\boldsymbol{t}\in\mathfrak{T}^{p}_{q}(\mathcal{N})
, then

(\varphi\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{T})(x)\left(\boldsymbol{a}^{\!\ast}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{a}^{\!\ast}_{p},\boldsymbol{w}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{w}_{q}\right)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\boldsymbol{T}(X)\left((\varphi\!\Downarrow\!\boldsymbol{a}^{\!\ast}_{1}),\ldots,(\varphi\!\Downarrow\!\boldsymbol{a}^{\!\ast}_{p}),(\varphi\!\Downarrow\!\boldsymbol{w}_{1}),\ldots,(\varphi\!\Downarrow\!\boldsymbol{w}_{q})\right)\,,

and

(\varphi\!\Downarrow\!\boldsymbol{t})(X)\left(\boldsymbol{B}^{\ast}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{B}^{\ast}_{p},\boldsymbol{V}_{\!1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{V}_{\!q}\right)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\boldsymbol{t}(x)\left((\varphi\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{B}^{\ast}_{1}),\ldots,(\varphi\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{B}^{\ast}_{p}),(\varphi\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{V}_{\!1}),\ldots,(\varphi\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{V}_{\!q})\right)\,,

where
X\in\mathcal{M}
and
x=\varphi(X)
.

Remark A.9
.

In general, pushforward and pullback do not commute with index
raising and lowering,
e.g.
\varphi\!\Uparrow\!(\boldsymbol{T}^{\flat})\neq(\varphi\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{T})^{\flat}
. In Proposition
2.5
, however, we show
that these operations indeed commute if the alteration (index
raising or lowering) is carried out by using the pushed or pulled metric tensor.

Definition A.30
.

An diffeomorphism
\varphi:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}

between Riemannian manifolds
(\mathcal{M},\boldsymbol{G})
and

(\mathcal{N},\boldsymbol{g})
is called an
isometry
if

\boldsymbol{g}=\varphi\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{G}\;,\qquad\mbox{or
equivalently,}\qquad\boldsymbol{G}(\boldsymbol{U},\boldsymbol{V})=\boldsymbol{g}(\varphi\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{U},\varphi\!\Uparrow\!\boldsymbol{V})\;,

for
\boldsymbol{U},\boldsymbol{V}\in\Upgamma(T\mathcal{M})
.

Proposition A.6
.

The tangent map of an isometry

\varphi:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}
is orthogonal, and
proper orthogonal, with
\det(T\varphi)=+1
, if
\varphi
is also
orientation-preserving.

Proof.

Let
\boldsymbol{G}
be the metric on
\mathcal{M}
, and
\boldsymbol{g}
the
metric on
\mathcal{N}
. Then, by Definition
A.27
and the
definition of an isometry,

\langle\boldsymbol{U},\boldsymbol{V}\rangle_{X}=\langle T\varphi\cdot\boldsymbol{U},T\varphi\cdot\boldsymbol{V}\rangle_{x=\varphi(X)}

for every
X\in\mathcal{M}
. On the other hand,
Definition
A.18
of the transpose yields

\langle T\varphi\cdot\boldsymbol{U},T\varphi\cdot\boldsymbol{V}\rangle_{x}=\langle(T\varphi)^{\mathrm{T}}\cdot(T\varphi\cdot\boldsymbol{U}),\boldsymbol{V}\rangle_{X}\;.

Comparison of both equations shows that

(T\varphi)^{\mathrm{T}}\cdot T\varphi=\boldsymbol{I}_{\mathcal{M}}\,,\qquad\mbox{that
is,}\qquad(T\varphi)^{-1}=(T\varphi)^{\mathrm{T}}\quad\mbox{at
every }X\in\mathcal{M}\,.

Proofing the second assertion requires the notion of orientation,
which is briefly introduced below.
∎

A.4
Tensor Analysis

Definition A.31
.

Let
\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w}\in\Upgamma(T\mathcal{N})
be vector fields on a
Riemannian manifold
\mathcal{N}
, and
\boldsymbol{v}
continuously
differentiable. In a chart
(\mathcal{V},\sigma)
on
\mathcal{N}

with coordinates
x^{i}
, the
covariant derivative of
\boldsymbol{v}
is the proper
\binom{1}{1}
-tensor
field defined through

\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{v}(x)=\nabla_{j}v^{i}(x)\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}x^{j}\otimes\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{i}}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\left(\frac{\partial v^{i}}{\partial x^{j}}+v^{k}\gamma^{\,\,\,i}_{k\,\,\,j}\right)\!(x)\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}x^{j}\otimes\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{i}}\;,

and the
covariant derivative of
\boldsymbol{v}
along
\boldsymbol{w}
is
the proper vector field defined through

\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\!\boldsymbol{w}}\boldsymbol{v}(x)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\boldsymbol{w}(x)\cdot\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{v}(x)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\left(\frac{\partial v^{i}}{\partial x^{j}}w^{j}+v^{k}w^{j}\gamma^{\,\,\,i}_{k\,\,\,j}\right)\!(x)\,\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{i}}\;.

If
\boldsymbol{a}^{\!\ast}\in\Upgamma(T^{\ast}\!\mathcal{N})
is
continuously differentiable, then

\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{a}^{\!\ast}\in\mathfrak{T}^{0}_{2}(\mathcal{N})
,
defined by

\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{a}^{\!\ast}(x)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\left(\frac{\partial a_{i}}{\partial x^{j}}-a_{k}\gamma^{\phantom{i}k\phantom{j}}_{i\phantom{k}j}\right)\!(x)\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}x^{i}\otimes\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}x^{j}\,,

is a proper
\binom{0}{2}
-tensor field. The term “proper” is
meant in the sense that the components of the covariant derivative
transform under chart transitions according to the tensorial
transformation rule (Proposition
A.2
). In particular,
under a chart transition such that
x^{i}\mapsto x^{i^{\prime}}
, the
connection coefficients transform according to

\gamma^{\phantom{k}j\phantom{i}}_{k\phantom{j}i}=\frac{\partial x^{k^{\prime}}}{\partial x^{k}}\frac{\partial x^{j}}{\partial x^{j^{\prime}}}\frac{\partial x^{i^{\prime}}}{\partial x^{i}}\;\gamma^{\phantom{k^{\prime}}j^{\prime}\phantom{i^{\prime}}}_{k^{\prime}\phantom{j^{\prime}}i^{\prime}}+\frac{\partial x^{j}}{\partial x^{m^{\prime}}}\frac{\partial^{2}x^{m^{\prime}}}{\partial x^{k}\partial x^{i}}\;,

with
i,i^{\prime},j,j^{\prime},k,k^{\prime},m,m^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,n_{\mathrm{dim}}\}
.

Definition A.32
.

The operator

\boldsymbol{\nabla}\!:\Upgamma(T\mathcal{N})\!\times\!\Upgamma(T\mathcal{N})\rightarrow\Upgamma(T\mathcal{N})

introduced in Definition
A.31
is referred to as the

connection
on
\mathcal{N}
, and

\gamma^{\phantom{k}j\phantom{i}}_{k\phantom{j}i}
are the

connection coefficients
. A connection
\boldsymbol{\nabla}
is
called
torsion-free
if

\gamma^{\phantom{k}j\phantom{i}}_{k\phantom{j}i}=\gamma^{\phantom{i}j\phantom{k}}_{i\phantom{j}k}
.
In case of a Riemannian manifold the connection coefficients are
called
Christoffel symbols of the second kind
.

Theorem A.1
.

Let
\mathcal{N}
be a Riemannian manifold, and
g_{ij}
and

g^{ij}
be the coefficients of the metric and inverse metric,
respectively, then there is a unique and torsion-free connection
whose coefficients are given by

\gamma^{\phantom{i}k\phantom{j}}_{i\phantom{k}j}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\frac{1}{2}g^{kl}\left(\frac{\partial g_{jl}}{\partial x^{i}}+\frac{\partial g_{il}}{\partial x^{j}}-\frac{\partial g_{ij}}{\partial x^{l}}\right)\,.

Proof.

Detailed derivations can be found, for example, in

[
19
,
66
,
110
]
.
∎

Proposition A.7
.

Let
\mathcal{N}
be a Riemannian manifold with connection

\boldsymbol{\nabla}
and metric
\boldsymbol{g}
, then

\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{g}=\boldsymbol{0}\,.

Proof.

Using the previous definitions, Proposition
A.1
, and

\boldsymbol{g}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}g_{ij}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}x^{i}\otimes\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}x^{j}
,

\nabla_{i}g_{jk}=\frac{\partial g_{jk}}{\partial x^{i}}-2g_{jl}\gamma^{\phantom{k}l\phantom{i}}_{k\phantom{l}i}=\frac{\partial g_{jk}}{\partial x^{i}}-\left(\frac{\partial g_{ij}}{\partial x^{k}}+\frac{\partial g_{kj}}{\partial x^{i}}-\frac{\partial g_{ki}}{\partial x^{j}}\right)=0\,.

∎

Proposition A.8
.

On a Riemannian manifold
\mathcal{N}
,

\boldsymbol{\nabla}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\flat})=(\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{u})^{\flat}
for any

\boldsymbol{u}\in\Upgamma(T\mathcal{N})
.

Proof.

This follows from a straightforward calculation by using
Proposition
A.7
.
∎

Definition A.33
.

The
divergence
of a tensor field

\boldsymbol{t}\in\mathfrak{T}^{p}_{q}(\mathcal{N})
is defined as the
contraction of its covariant derivative
\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{t}
on the
last contravariant leg. For examples, if
\boldsymbol{t}
is a

\binom{3}{0}
-tensor field, then

\left(\mathrm{div}\,\boldsymbol{t}\right)^{ij}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\nabla_{k}\,t^{ijk}\;.

Proposition A.9
.

Let
\mathcal{N}
be a Riemannian manifold with metric
coefficients
g_{ij}
in a positively oriented chart and

\boldsymbol{v}\in\Upgamma(T\mathcal{N})
a vector field, then

\mathrm{div}\,\boldsymbol{v}=\nabla_{i}\,v^{i}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\det\,g_{kl}|}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}\left(\sqrt{|\det\,g_{kl}|}\;v^{i}\right)\,.

Proof.

By Definition
A.31
and Proposition
A.1
; see

[
4
]
for details.
∎

Weak or variational formulations of balance of momentum (principle of virtual power) often employ the product rule for the divergence, which will be derived here for a particular case.

Proposition A.10
.

Let
\boldsymbol{v}\in\Upgamma(T\!\mathcal{N})
and
\boldsymbol{T}\in\mathfrak{T}^{1}_{1}(\mathcal{N})
, then the following product rule holds:

\mathrm{div}(\boldsymbol{T}\cdot\boldsymbol{v})=\mathrm{div}\,\boldsymbol{T}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}+\boldsymbol{T}:\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{v}\;.

Proof.

By choosing a local chart on
\mathcal{N}
with coordinates
x^{i}
and making use of the properties of the Kronecker delta, Definitions
A.31
and
A.33
yield

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}(\boldsymbol{T}\cdot\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{i}(T^{i}_{\phantom{i}j}v^{j})=(\nabla_{i}T^{i}_{\phantom{i}j})\,v^{l}\delta^{j}_{\phantom{j}l}+T^{k}_{\phantom{k}j}(\nabla_{i}v^{l})\,\delta^{i}_{\phantom{i}k}\delta^{j}_{\phantom{j}l}

\displaystyle=(\nabla_{i}T^{i}_{\phantom{i}j})v^{l}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}x^{j}\cdot\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{l}}\right)+T^{k}_{\phantom{k}j}(\nabla_{i}v^{l})\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{k}}\otimes\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}x^{j}\right)\!:\!\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}x^{i}\otimes\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{l}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\nabla_{i}T^{i}_{\phantom{i}j}\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}x^{j}\right)\cdot\left(v^{l}\,\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{l}}\right)+\left(T^{k}_{\phantom{k}j}\,\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{k}}\otimes\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}x^{j}\right):\left(\nabla_{i}v^{l}\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}x^{i}\otimes\frac{\boldsymbol{\partial}}{\boldsymbol{\partial}x^{l}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{div}\,\boldsymbol{T}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}+\boldsymbol{T}:\boldsymbol{\nabla}\boldsymbol{v}\;.

∎

Definition A.34
.

The evolution in time of a differentiable manifold
\mathcal{N}

is described by a mapping

\psi_{t,s}:\mathcal{N}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}
, where
t,s
are
points in a time interval
\mathcal{I}\subset\mathbb{R}
. The
mapping
\psi_{t,s}
is called a
time-dependent flow on

\mathcal{N}
provided that

\psi_{t,s}\circ\psi_{s,r}=\psi_{t,r}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\psi_{t,t}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{N}}\,.

Remark A.10
.

If
X_{s}=\psi_{s,s}(X_{s})\in\mathcal{N}
is the starting point at
starting time
t=s
, then
X=c(t)=\psi_{t,s}(X_{s})=\psi(X_{s},s,t)

is the point at
t=t
, for
s,t
fixed. Hence,

c:\mathcal{I}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}
is a curve on
\mathcal{N}

with the initial condition
c(s)=X_{s}
. The flow
\psi_{t,s}
is
closely connected to a time-dependent vector field

\boldsymbol{u}:\mathcal{N}\times\mathcal{I}\rightarrow T\mathcal{N}

through
\boldsymbol{u}(\psi_{t,s}(X_{s}),t)=\dot{c}(t)
, with
c(s)=X_{s}
.
Conversely,
c(t)
is the unique integral curve of
\boldsymbol{u}

starting at
X_{s}
at time
t=s
; thus
\boldsymbol{u}
generates the flow,
so
\psi_{t,s}
needs not to be given explicitly.

Definition A.35
.

The
Lie derivative
of a time-dependent tensor field

\boldsymbol{T}_{\!t}\in\mathfrak{T}^{p}_{q}(\mathcal{N})
along a
time-dependent vector field
\boldsymbol{u}_{t}\in\Upgamma(T\mathcal{N})
is
defined by

\mathrm{L}_{\boldsymbol{u}}\boldsymbol{T}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\lim_{\mathrm{\Delta}t\rightarrow 0}\frac{\psi_{t,s}\!\Downarrow\!\boldsymbol{T}_{t}-\boldsymbol{T}_{s}}{\mathrm{\Delta}t}=\lim_{\mathrm{\Delta}t\rightarrow 0}\frac{\psi_{t,s}\!\Downarrow\!\boldsymbol{T}_{t}-\psi_{s,s}\!\Downarrow\!\boldsymbol{T}_{s}}{\mathrm{\Delta}t}=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\psi_{t,s}\!\Downarrow\!\boldsymbol{T}_{t}\right|_{t=s}\,,

where
\mathrm{\Delta}t\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}t-s
, and

\psi_{t,s}\!\Downarrow
denotes the pullback concerning the flow

\psi_{t,s}
associated with
\boldsymbol{u}_{t}
. Therefore, the Lie
derivative approximately answers the question how a tensor field

\boldsymbol{T}
changes under some flow. The so-called
autonomous
Lie derivative
is obtained by holding
t
fixed in

\boldsymbol{T}_{\!t}
, that is,

\pounds_{\boldsymbol{u}}\boldsymbol{T}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\lim_{\mathrm{\Delta}t\rightarrow 0}\frac{\psi^{\star}_{t,s}\boldsymbol{T}_{s}-\psi^{\star}_{s,s}\boldsymbol{T}_{s}}{\mathrm{\Delta}t}=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\psi^{\star}_{t,s}\boldsymbol{T}_{s}\right|_{t=s}=\mathrm{L}_{\boldsymbol{u}}\boldsymbol{T}-\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{T}}{\partial t}\;.

If
\boldsymbol{T}
is time-independent,

\mathrm{L}_{\boldsymbol{u}}\boldsymbol{T}\equiv\pounds_{\boldsymbol{u}}\boldsymbol{T}
.
Fig.
4
illustrates the concept.

Figure 4:
Lie derivative of a time-independent vector field

\boldsymbol{v}
along a time-dependent vector field
\boldsymbol{u}
; reprint
from
[
4
, fig. 3.5]

Proposition A.11
.

The Lie derivative of the tensor field
\boldsymbol{T}_{\!t}
along the
vector field
\boldsymbol{u}_{t}
is obtained by pulling back the tensor
field according to the flow associated with
\boldsymbol{u}_{t}
at some
starting time, performing the common time derivative, and then
pushing forward the result using the inverse of the pullback.
Clearly,

\mathrm{L}_{\boldsymbol{u}}\boldsymbol{T}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\psi_{t,s}\!\Uparrow\!\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}(\psi_{t,s}\!\Downarrow\!\boldsymbol{T}_{\!t})\;.

Proof.

We refer to
[
4
]
and
[
1
, sect. 5.4]
for a
detailed discussion.
∎

Proposition A.12
.

Let
\varphi:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}
be a
diffeomorphism,
\boldsymbol{u}_{t},\boldsymbol{v}_{t}\in\Upgamma(T\mathcal{N})
, and

\boldsymbol{T}\in\mathfrak{T}^{p}_{q}(\mathcal{N})
, then

\pounds_{\boldsymbol{u}+\boldsymbol{v}}=\pounds_{\boldsymbol{u}}+\pounds_{\boldsymbol{v}}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\varphi\!\Downarrow\!(\pounds_{\boldsymbol{u}}\boldsymbol{T})=\pounds_{(\varphi\Downarrow\boldsymbol{u})}\,(\varphi\!\Downarrow\!\boldsymbol{T})\;.

Proof.

See, for example,
[
66
]
.
∎

Proposition A.13
.

Let
\mathcal{N}
be a Riemannian manifold, and

\boldsymbol{u}_{t}\in\Upgamma(T\mathcal{N})
be a time-dependent vector
field. In a chart
(\mathcal{V},\sigma)
on
\mathcal{N}
with
coordinates
x^{i}
, the components of the Lie derivative of a
time-dependent tensor field

\boldsymbol{T}_{\!t}\in\mathfrak{T}^{1}_{1}(\mathcal{M})
along
\boldsymbol{u}_{t}

are computed from

(\mathrm{L}_{\boldsymbol{u}}\boldsymbol{T})^{i}_{\phantom{i}j}=\frac{\partial T^{i}_{\phantom{i}j}}{\partial t}+u^{k}\frac{\partial T^{i}_{\phantom{i}j}}{\partial x^{k}}-T^{k}_{\phantom{k}j}\frac{\partial u^{i}}{\partial x^{k}}+T^{i}_{\phantom{i}k}\frac{\partial u^{k}}{\partial x^{j}}\,.

If
\mathcal{N}
has a torsion-free connection
\boldsymbol{\nabla}
, then

(\mathrm{L}_{\boldsymbol{u}}\boldsymbol{T})^{i}_{\phantom{i}j}=\frac{\partial T^{i}_{\phantom{i}j}}{\partial t}+u^{k}\nabla_{k}T^{i}_{\phantom{i}j}-\nabla_{k}T^{k}_{\phantom{k}j}u^{i}+\nabla_{j}T^{i}_{\phantom{i}k}u^{k}\,.

Proof.

We refer again to
[
66
]
for detailed proof.
∎

Remark A.11
.

As pushforward and pullback do not commute with index raising and
lowering, the Lie derivative also does not commute with these
operations in general, that is, for example,

\mathrm{L}_{\boldsymbol{u}}(\boldsymbol{T}^{\flat})\neq(\mathrm{L}_{\boldsymbol{u}}\boldsymbol{T})^{\flat}
.

From now on, we let
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{N}
be
Riemannian manifolds with some orientation,

\varphi:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}
be an
orientation-preserving diffeomorphism,
(\mathcal{U},\beta)
be a
positively oriented chart of
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathcal{M}
with
respect to the orientation of
\mathcal{M}
, and

(\mathcal{V},\sigma)
be a positively oriented chart of

\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{N}
, with non-empty

\varphi^{-1}(\mathcal{V})\cap\mathcal{U}\subset\mathcal{M}
.
Furthermore, let

\varphi^{i}(X^{I})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(x^{i}\circ\varphi\circ\beta^{-1})(X^{I})

be the coordinates
x^{i}
on
\mathcal{N}
arising from the
coordinates
X^{I}
on
\mathcal{U}
via localization of
\varphi
.

Remark A.12
.

A precise definition of
orientation
requires exterior
calculus, which is probably one of the most exotic fields of
modern differential geometry, at least from an engineer’s point of
view. The interested reader is referred to the literature
suggested at the beginning of this appendix.

Definition A.36
.

Let the tuple
(\boldsymbol{w}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{w}_{n})
of vector fields

\boldsymbol{w}_{1},

\ldots,\boldsymbol{w}_{n}\in\Upgamma(T\mathcal{N})
be
positively oriented with respect to the orientation of

\mathcal{N}
, then the
volume density
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}v}
on

\mathcal{N}
is defined through

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}v}(\boldsymbol{w}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{w}_{n})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\sqrt{\det\!\left\langle\boldsymbol{w}_{i},\boldsymbol{w}_{j}\right\rangle}\;,

where
\det\langle\boldsymbol{w}_{i},\boldsymbol{w}_{j}\rangle
is the determinant of
the matrix
(W_{ij})
whose elements are given by the inner
products

W_{ij}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\langle\boldsymbol{w}_{i},\boldsymbol{w}_{j}\rangle
.

Remark A.13
.

Note that
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}v}(\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{n})\!=\!1
for a
positively oriented ortho-normalized basis

\{\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{n}\}
in
T\mathcal{N}
. In ordinary

\mathbb{R}^{3}
, the volume of the parallelepiped spanned by the
three vectors
\boldsymbol{w}_{1},\boldsymbol{w}_{2},\boldsymbol{w}_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
is
given by

V(\boldsymbol{w}_{1},\boldsymbol{w}_{2},\boldsymbol{w}_{3})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\sqrt{\det\!\left\langle\boldsymbol{w}_{i},\boldsymbol{w}_{j}\right\rangle}

provided that
\boldsymbol{w}_{1},\boldsymbol{w}_{2}
, and
\boldsymbol{w}_{3}
are
positively oriented.

Proposition A.14
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}V}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}v}
be the volume densities on

\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{N}
, respectively, then

\varphi\!\Downarrow\!\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}v}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}v}\circ\varphi=J_{\varphi}\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}V}\;,

where

J_{\varphi}(X)=\det\left(\frac{\partial\varphi^{i}}{\partial X^{I}}\right)\frac{\sqrt{\det\,g_{ij}(\varphi(X))}}{\sqrt{\det\,G_{IJ}(X)}}

using local coordinates.

Proof.

The proof is most easily obtained using local representatives of

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}V}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}v}
; cf.
[
4
,
66
]
.
∎

Definition A.37
.

The proper scalar field

J_{\varphi}:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
introduced by
Proposition
A.14
is called the
Jacobian of

\varphi
or
relative volume change
with respect to

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}V}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}v}
. Since
\varphi
was assumed
orientation-preserving,
J_{\varphi}>0
.

Proposition A.15
.

(See
[
1
,
66
]
for a proof.)
\pounds_{\boldsymbol{u}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}v}=(\mathrm{div}\,\boldsymbol{u})\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}v}
.

Theorem A.2

(Change of Variables)
.

Let
f:\varphi(\mathcal{M})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, then

\int_{\varphi(\mathcal{M})}f\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}v}=\int_{\mathcal{M}}\varphi\!\Downarrow\!(f\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}v})=\int_{\mathcal{M}}(f\circ\varphi)J_{\varphi}\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}V}\;.

Proof.

This is well-known from the analysis of real functions. The last expression is a consequence of Proposition
A.14
.
∎

The following relations, including the divergence theorem, play a
fundamental role in both differential geometry and continuum
mechanics. A full derivation is beyond the scope of this paper and
can be found elsewhere, e.g.
[
1
,
4
,
66
]
.

Definition A.38
.

Let
\mathcal{N}
be an oriented
n
-dimensional manifold with
compatible oriented boundary
\partial\mathcal{N}
such that the

normals to
\partial\mathcal{N}
,

\boldsymbol{n}^{\!\ast}\in\Upgamma(T^{\ast}\!\mathcal{N})
, point

outwards
. The
area density

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}a}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}v}_{\partial\mathcal{N}}
is
the volume density on
(n-1)
-dimensional
\partial\mathcal{N}

induced by the volume density
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}v}
on
\mathcal{N}
.
Conceptually, we write
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}v}=\boldsymbol{n}^{\!\ast}\!\wedge\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}a}
to emphasize that
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}v}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}a}
are linked by
the outward normals.

Theorem A.3

(Divergence Theorem)
.

Let
\boldsymbol{w}\in\Upgamma(T\mathcal{N})
be a vector field, then for
the situation defined above,

\int_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathrm{div}\,\boldsymbol{w})\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}v}=\int_{\partial\mathcal{N}}\boldsymbol{w}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{\!\ast}\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}a}\,.

Proposition A.16
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be oriented and

\varphi:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}
an orientation
preserving diffeomorphism with tangent
\boldsymbol{F}=T\varphi
. Let

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}A}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}a}
be the volume forms on

\partial\mathcal{M}
and
\partial(\varphi(\mathcal{M}))
,
respectively. Then
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}a}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}A}\circ\varphi^{-1}
if and
only if the outward normals on
\partial(\varphi(\mathcal{M}))

and
\partial\mathcal{M}
are related by

\boldsymbol{n}^{\!\ast}=\varphi_{\star}(J_{\varphi}\boldsymbol{N}^{\ast})=(J_{\varphi}\circ\varphi^{-1})\boldsymbol{F}^{-\mathrm{T}}\!\cdot(\boldsymbol{N}^{\ast}\circ\varphi^{-1})\;,

where
\boldsymbol{n}^{\ast}\in\Upgamma(T^{\ast}\!\mathcal{N})
,

\boldsymbol{N}^{\ast}\in\Upgamma(T^{\ast}\!\mathcal{M})
, and
J_{\varphi}
is
the Jacobian of
\varphi
.

Definition A.39
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
,
\varphi
, etc., be as before, then the

Piola transform
of a spatial vector field

\boldsymbol{y}\in\Upgamma(T\mathcal{N})
is the vector field on

\mathcal{M}
given by

\boldsymbol{Y}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}J_{\varphi}\,\varphi\!\Downarrow\!\boldsymbol{y}=J_{\varphi}\boldsymbol{F}^{-1}\!\cdot(\boldsymbol{y}\circ\varphi)\quad\in\Upgamma(T\mathcal{M})\;.

Theorem A.4

(Piola Identity)
.

If
\boldsymbol{Y}
is the Piola transform of
\boldsymbol{y}
, then the
divergence operators
\mathrm{DIV}
on
\mathcal{M}
and

\mathrm{div}
on
\mathcal{N}
are related by

\mathrm{DIV}\,\boldsymbol{Y}=(\mathrm{div}\,\boldsymbol{y}\circ\varphi)\,J_{\varphi}\;.

Proposition A.17
.

\int_{\partial\varphi(\mathcal{M})}\boldsymbol{y}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{\!\ast}\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}a}=\int_{\partial\mathcal{M}}\boldsymbol{Y}\!\cdot\boldsymbol{N}^{\!\ast}\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}A}\qquad\mbox{resp.}\qquad(\boldsymbol{y}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}^{\!\ast}\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}a})\circ\varphi=\boldsymbol{Y}\!\cdot\boldsymbol{N}^{\!\ast}\,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}A}\;.
